| D76 | **x86-64 SSE2 kernels** (P3.02d, `docs/research/string-speed.md`): `libc/string/x86_64` (`LIBC_STRING_KERNELS_speed := x86_64`; the size flavour stays the C code). `memcpy`/`memset`: overlapping head/tail moves for 0–32 bytes (8/4/2/1 and 16-byte pairs, all loads before stores), above 32 the first and last 16 bytes loaded first and stored last around an aligned 32-byte loop; `memmove` forward and up to 32 through `memcpy`, otherwise mirrored downwards. `strlen`/`strchr`: aligned 16-byte blocks (never across a page) with `PCMPEQB`/`PMOVMSKB`, the bits below the start shifted out, an empty-string or two-byte prologue. `strcmp`: 8 byte pairs first (5 instructions each), then unaligned 16-byte blocks while both pointers are at most 4080 bytes into their page, flagged by `PCMPEQB`, `PMINUB`, `PCMPEQB` against zero. x86-64: `memcpy` 1024 B 1463 → 296 instructions aligned, 6184 → 303 misaligned, the small-copy mix 338 → 40, `strcmp` 1024 B 7203 → 1288, `strlen` 1064 → 426; 483 cases faster, none slower. `test/unit/string_kernels` now covers offsets modulo 16, strings across a page boundary and bytes before the start. | SSE2 is the x86-64 baseline, so the reference target gets kernels shaped like the ones production libraries use; prologues sized by measurement keep the short cases at or below the C code's cost. |
| D77 | **Speed files per module; integer conversions** (P3.03a, `docs/research/conversions-speed.md`): a module may hold a `speed/` directory whose files replace the generic files of the same name in the speed flavour (`subdir.mk` drops them when `LIBC_FLAVOUR` is `speed`, `libc/libc.mk` archives `libc/stdio/speed` and `libc/stdlib/speed` then; `speed.mk` exports `LIBC_FLAVOUR`); the size flavour stays byte-identical to phase 2, and a flavour archive still replaces members by name. Speed `__libc_strtoint`: 32-bit accumulation with no check below 2^32 / 36, then `ckd_mul`/`ckd_add` in 32 bits for a 32-bit limit or unchecked 64-bit below 2^64 / 36 for a wider one, one limit test at the end, no division. Speed `__libc_format_digits`: decimal above 32 bits by 10^9 chunks (two 64-bit divisions at most) written in pairs per division by 100, power-of-two bases shifting a 32-bit copy once the value fits, the rest unchanged (a `noinline` helper keeps the common path's code as it was). Speed `__libc_format_buffer_write`: `memcpy` (a tail call) from 16 bytes. `__libc_scan` at `LIBC_SPEED_CFLAGS`; `__libc_format` stays `-Os` (at `-O2` every printf image breaks the speed budget). armv7e-m: `strtol` 10 digits 1213 → 305, `strtoll` 1496 → 516, `%lld` 7636 → 1353, a literal 512 → 308, `sscanf` mix 2081 → 1624; armv6-m `%lld` 11025 → 3702, `strtol("42")` 914 → 170; no case slower. 32-bit `%d` on Armv6-M still divides per digit (an Armv6-M speed file, later). | The size budget allows the size flavour no growth, so speed code has to be selectable per file; the conversions' cost was 64-bit division and 64-bit arithmetic that short inputs never need. |
| D78 | **A middle path for decimal to binary floating point** (P3.03b, `docs/research/conversions-speed.md`; amends D38 in the speed flavour): `libc/stdlib/speed/__libc_strtofloat.c` replaces the parser. It tests digits in line instead of calling `isdigit`, and Clinger's guard multiplies (`significand * 10 <= limit`) instead of dividing 2^precision by ten. Between Clinger's fast path (still first: one floating-point operation beats the middle path on every board, software floating point included) and Simple Decimal Conversion it adds an Eisel–Lemire-style path for up to 19 significant digits, q in [-364, 335], in any rounding direction. 5^q is rebuilt as 5^(28j) (25 rows, normalised and truncated to 128 bits, scale 127 - 65j + (j < 0)) times 5^r (28 exact 64-bit rows), 624 bytes in all instead of Eisel–Lemire's 10 KiB. Two 128 × 64 products keep the top 128 bits from 32-bit multiplications. An explicit bound (below the value by under 10 units of the last bit, plus 1/w with dropped digits) must not start on or reach a half-ulp boundary at the format's precision. The high word then goes to `__libc_float_pack`, which rounds in any direction, subnormals included. Ties and near-ties fall back. armv7e-m: `DBL_MIN` 86186 → 1439, `6.02e300` 170760 → 1314, 17 digits 4472 → 1377, `"3.14"` 1962 → 1137; armv6-m: `DBL_MIN` 1839969 → 3853; no case slower. The size flavour is unchanged. | A full power table is too large for the targets, and Simple Decimal Conversion cost up to 1.8 million instructions; a compact table with an explicit error bound keeps exactness checkable and the fallback rare. |
| D79 | **The lock word in the seam contract** (P3.04a, `docs/research/locks.md`; amends D31): `struct __rtos_lock` starts with an `unsigned int` lock word, 0 when free, else the owner's token with `__RTOS_LOCK_SLOW` set when releasing needs the port (nested holds, or a thread may be waiting). A thread's token is its value of `thread_local unsigned int __rtos_lock_token`, which the port defines (nonzero, even, unique among threads that may lock). The port can make it name the thread, as a PI futex's TID does, for priority inheritance. Anyone may take a free lock by a compare-and-swap of 0 to the token and give back a lock held once by a compare-and-swap back to 0; `__rtos_lock_acquire`/`release` must accept such a word at any time, and a single-threaded port may keep no-op functions. The Linux port does exactly that as its fast path. Its slow path counts recursion in a depth field (the owner marks the word with a plain store) and waits on the word as a futex: a waiter marks it and takes it marked. It needs neither `__rtos_thread_self` nor an owner pointer. The initial thread's token is 2, and each new thread takes the next even number at start. libc keeps one call per operation in both flavours. An in-line compare-and-swap at each call site measured 92.5 → 67.5 instructions per malloc + free on armv7e-m, but cost 25–35 bytes per site and broke the speed budget by up to 200 bytes per malloc or stdio image (Q16), so the contract allows it but libc does not use it yet. Result in both flavours, malloc + free: armv7e-m 107.5 → 92.5, x86-64 107 → 84, armv6-m 233 → 224. Images lose 0–84 bytes of code and 36–96 bytes of RAM; the Armv6-M acquire frame grows 32 → 40 bytes (justified in `docs/size/armv6-m.md`). | Locking was 57 % of an allocation on armv7e-m; a lock word that both sides understand takes the common case out of the port's slow path without fixing the port's lock type, and keeps priority inheritance possible. |
| D80 | **No allocator fast path beyond the lock** (P3.04b, `docs/research/malloc-speed.md`): small-size-class bins in front of nano's address-ordered first fit were measured in the speed flavour and rejected. Twelve exact-size LIFO bins, flushed on failure, gave `fixed/16` −12 % and `batch/shuffled` −8 % on armv7e-m, but `batch/fifo` +11 % and `realloc/grow` +117 %, because unmerged chunks fragment the list and defeat in-place growth. Consolidating them before larger requests (glibc's `malloc_consolidate`) was worse (+17–61 %). Three bins still cost `realloc/grow` +48–134 %. TLSF in the speed flavour was 70–170 % slower (about 200 instructions a call against 90); nano at `-O2` gained nothing on Arm. With short free lists, nano already does a few list steps a call, and the lock fast path (D79) was the fast path that paid. TLSF stays the choice for bounded time on heaps with many free chunks. | Measured: deferred coalescing trades a small win on one size for fragmentation and lost `realloc` growth on mixed sizes. |
| D81 | **x86-64 stays at the SSE2 baseline** (P3.05a): `x86_64` is the test host and the performance reference, so its code must run on any x86-64 and does not assume SSE4.1's `roundsd`. `sqrt`/`sqrtf` already use `sqrtsd`/`sqrtss` (the `x86_64` libm layer). The SSE2 rounding alternative, a `cvttsd2si` round trip, raises inexact, which `floor`, `ceil`, `trunc` and `round` must not (libc-test checks the flags). Saving and restoring MXCSR around it costs more than the generic bit manipulation (about 30 instructions), so the rounding functions stay generic. | The reference target must not need a newer ISA than the code it measures against; no faster correct SSE2 sequence exists. |
| D82 | **What the FPU computes, for the private libm code** (P3.05b, `docs/research/libm-fma.md`): `libc/internal/fpu/<LIBC_FPU>/libc-fpu.h` (on `CPPFLAGS` from the toolchain files; x86-64 exports `LIBC_FPU := sse2`) declares `constexpr bool __libc_fpu_fmaf`, true where `fmaf` is one instruction (FPv4-SP, FPv5-SP, FPv5-DP). `__libc_ff_product` then returns `{ a·b, fma(a, b, -a·b) }` instead of Dekker's split products. Both are exact under the same conditions, so every float-float result is unchanged bit for bit (D57–D68 stand), and the constant folds the other branch away (no `#if`). armv7e-m and armv8-m.main, both flavours: `erff` 2539 → 1384, `expf` 228 → 161, `logf` 234 → 166, `tgammaf` 1198 → 847, `sinf` 694 → 576, 54 cases faster, none slower; `libc.a` −4.3 KiB and `math-float` 5282 → 4818 bytes on armv7e-m. Measured and not taken: `scalbnf`-free reduction halves (−21 instructions in `sinf`, but +8–12 bytes on Armv6-M); a float Cody–Waite reduction (cancellation leaves about 2^-22 relative error at the smallest \|d\| of 2^-30, found by an exhaustive count over every float); `-O2` for the layer (mixed, over the Armv6-M speed budget). | One instruction replaces seventeen in the operation every float-float function repeats, with no change to any result. |
| D83 | **Double-double products by fma on FPv5-DP** (P3.05c, `docs/research/libm-fma.md`; extends D82): `libc-fpu.h` adds `constexpr bool __libc_fpu_fma` (true on FPv5-DP only), and `__libc_dd_product` returns `{ a·b, fma(a, b, -a·b) }` there instead of Dekker's split products: exact under the same conditions, so every double result (and every float result computed in double on that core) is unchanged bit for bit; the cores already work in round-to-nearest (D47). armv7e-m-dp, both flavours: `asin` 337 → 221, `tan` 307 → 208, `sin` 219 → 166, `log` 190 → 151, `lgamma` 616 → 500; 102 cases faster, none slower; `libc.a` −3.4 KiB. The polynomials are not re-tuned: their error budget is set by the double-double steps, whose results the change leaves bit-identical, so fewer terms would need a new error analysis per core. | The same one-instruction exact product as D82, for the double cores of the one board with a double-precision FPU. |
| D84 | **No Cortex-M7-specific code without cycle measurements** (P3.05d): the speed gate is qemu's instruction count (D72), which sees neither the M7's dual issue nor its 64-bit AXI/TCM path. The Thumb-2 `memcpy` (D73) already moves 32 bytes per `LDM`/`STM` pair; `LDRD` moves 8 bytes per instruction, and `VLDM` of four D registers matches the 32 bytes but adds FPU register use (and lazy-stacking cost in an interrupt's context). So nothing an instruction count can confirm is left to change. An armv7e-m-dp variant tuned for the M7 pipeline waits for DWT cycle counts on hardware through `port/none` (Q5); the `fpv5-dp` layer stays shared with any later Armv8.1-M DP core (Q10). | Without hardware, a pipeline-tuned variant would be unmeasured code: it could not be gated, and it could be slower on the cores it does not target. |
| D85 | **Integer float multiplication, division and square root on Armv6-M** (P3.05e, `docs/research/softfloat.md`; answers Q14 in part). The `nofp` layer (armv6-m, armv6s-m) defines `__aeabi_fmul`, `__aeabi_fdiv` and `sqrtf` in integers. They share `libc/math/nofp/softfloat.h`: unpack, with subnormals normalised, and one rounding to nearest, ties to even, from a guard and a sticky bit (D44: the only direction without an FPU, with no flags). `fmul` builds the 48-bit significand product from four 12-bit half products, since Armv6-M multiplies 32 × 32 → 32. `fdiv` is a 26-step restoring division. `sqrtf` is a 25-step digit recurrence whose remainder stays below 2^28, so it uses no 64-bit arithmetic. Every float function of the `sp-nearest` layer gains: `sqrtf` 2036 → 461, `hypotf` −25 %, `erff` −7 %, `expf` −3 % (62 cases faster, none slower). The helpers are 340 and 296 bytes against libgcc's 504 and 512. Each has a 32-byte frame, which adds 24 bytes of stack to `sinf`, `expf` and `powf`. `__aeabi_fadd` stays libgcc's: an integer one was exact but made 51 cases slower. The step's larger target, fixed-point `expf`/`logf`/`sinf`/`erff`, is split into P3.05f–h. | Multiplication and division are where integer code beats libgcc's generic soft-fp on this core; addition is not. Every float function calls these helpers, so replacing them speeds up the whole layer at once, with exactness easy to test, before the per-function rewrites. |
| D86 | **Fixed-point exponentials and logarithms in the Armv6-M speed flavour** (P3.05f, `docs/research/libm-fixed.md`). `libc/math/nofp/speed` holds `expf`, `exp2f`, `logf` and `log2f`. `libc/libc.mk` archives a layer's `speed/` directory in the speed flavour, and `sp-nearest/subdir.mk` then drops its files of the same name (D77 extended to the libm layers); the size flavour is unchanged. Each function first computes 2^n (1 + F 2^-32) in 32-bit fixed point. The exponentials use a 32-entry 2^(j/32) table and a degree-4 polynomial. The logarithms use the D58 reduction, made exact as a Q62 product, and a degree-5 series in signed Q31 that stays relative near 1. F is rounded at once unless its 9 low bits lie within an error bound of the halfway point (Ziv's test). Otherwise the float-float code, kept in the same function, gives the size flavour's result. The bounds (6 and 8 units of 2^-32) are above the largest errors measured by running the core code on the host over every float argument (2.71 and 4.49), with no wrong fast-path result. `exp2f` 9269 → 316, `expf` 11034 → 375, `log2f` 10547 → 583, `logf` 12367 → 2210 (two of the benchmark's 16 arguments fall back). One shared 64-bit product helper and split tables keep `math-float` within the +10 % speed budget (10520 of 10604 bytes). | Integer arithmetic replaces about 70 soft-float calls with about 15 multiplications of 16-bit halves. Ziv's test keeps the D57 results exactly, by reusing the existing code as the accurate path, without a second accurate implementation. An exhaustive check is possible in binary32, so the error bounds are proven, not estimated. |
| D87 | **Fixed-point sine, cosine and tangent on Armv6-M; a larger speed budget for the math images** (P3.05g, `docs/research/libm-fixed.md`; extends D86, amends the speed margin of D71/D73). `libc/math/nofp/speed` adds `sinf`, `cosf` and `tanf`. They keep the integer reduction (D60) and its float-float s, and compute sin(kπ/32 + s) with `__libc_sinf_fixed`: s in units of 2^-48, cos s − 1 and sin s/s − 1 in Q31, a 9-entry sin/cos table in units of 2^-62, and the D60 quadrant logic. `tanf` divides the sine by the cosine to 33 bits with a correction for the cosine's low half. Ziv's test then falls back to the float-float code, as in D86. The bounds (3 units for sin and cos, 4 for tan) are above the largest errors measured over every argument on the host (1.29 and 1.39). `sinf` 18350 → 4729, `cosf` 18281 → 3742, `tanf` 36223 → 5140. The speed flavour's `math-float` grows to 11460 bytes, over the +10 % budget (10604). By the user's choice, `docs/size/budget.json` now holds per-program margins (`program_margins_percent`): `math-float` and `math-mix` may grow by +20 % in the speed flavour. Every other image keeps +10 %, and the size flavour +0 %. | A 4–7× faster `sinf`/`cosf`/`tanf` was worth 856 bytes in the one image that links them; the user preferred raising the math images' speed margin to leaving them out. The size flavour, the budget of record for code size, is untouched. |
| D88 | **Fixed-point error functions on Armv6-M** (P3.05h, `docs/research/libm-fixed.md`; extends D86). `libc/math/nofp/speed` adds `erff` and `erfcf` over `__libc_erff_fixed`. Below 2: erf(x0 + h) = erf(x0) + g(x0)·h·(1 + S), with nodes i/8, g = (2/√π)e^(-x0²), and S the integral series of e^(-2x0t − t²). Its coefficients come from the recurrence (k + 1)q_{k+1} = −2x0h·q_k − 2h²·q_{k−1}. At the node 0, erf is formed from x's significand. From 2 to 9.25: erfc = e^(-x²)·R, with R = erfc·e^(x²) Taylor-stepped from nodes 2 + i/8 by the recurrence of R' = 2xR − 2/√π, and e^(-x²) from the D86 exp2 core. The tables hold about 1 KB. Every series step is an exact product rounded to nearest, in Q32, or Q35 for R. The bound (8 units of 2^-32) is above the largest errors measured over every argument on the host (3.92 and 5.69). `erff` 141024 → 2967 and `erfcf` 119604 → 2874 instructions, with Ziv's test before the float-float code as in D86. | The Taylor coefficients come from each function's differential equation, so each node needs only two values, where piecewise rational fits would need a coefficient set per interval. The exhaustive check found three precision losses that the error estimate had missed. |
| D89 | **Phase 3 close-out** (P3.06, `docs/perf/phase-3.md`). The final report compares both flavours with the phase 2 baseline (P3.01, commit 75cd261) and, for context, with picolibc 1.8.6 and newlib-nano 4.4.0. That comparison is `bench-runner compare`: the same `test/bench` programs, built against the packages `size-report compare` pins, with Linux shims in `tools/bench-compare`, and counted by the same qemu plugin. Speed flavour against phase 2, geometric means: string 0.52–0.89, strto 0.13–0.26, malloc 0.77–0.93, math 0.65 (Armv6-M) and 0.84–0.87 (FPU cores). The size flavour is 0.1–8.9 % smaller in every image, and both budgets hold. Against the other libraries: faster in string, strto and malloc than picolibc; 2–3 times the instructions in math (correct rounding) and qsort (heapsort, Q17). | The phase's gains are measured against the baseline taken before any of its changes. The comparison with other libraries uses the same programs, compiler and counter, so the counts are comparable even though the libraries' accuracy is not. |
| D90 | **An introsort `qsort` in the speed flavour** (P3.07, `docs/research/qsort-speed.md`; answers Q17). `libc/stdlib/speed/qsort.c` first checks the whole input for a run: an input in order or all equal costs n − 1 comparisons, and a strictly descending one n − 1 and a reversal. Any other input goes to an introsort (Musser): Hoare partitions around a median of three, or a ninther above 64 elements, with both scans stopping on keys equal to the pivot; insertion sort at 8 elements or fewer; a heapsort for a range past its 2·log2 n depth budget. Iterative, with the larger part stacked, so at most one range per bit of `size_t` waits; pointer arithmetic throughout; swaps by word, by words or by bytes. The size flavour keeps the heapsort (D33). Speed flavour, armv7e-m: random input 640 → 274 instructions per element (picolibc 278, newlib-nano 277), strings 585 → 303, ordered inputs 27–36; `qsort.o` +625 bytes, stack depth 68 → 508 bytes. | Quicksort's 1.4 n log2 n comparisons beat heapsort's 2 n log2 n when each comparison is a call. The depth limit keeps D33's O(n log n) worst case, which a test with McIlroy's adversary checks. The run check pays for the inputs where heapsort was linear. |
| D91 | **A comparison phase before the toolchain; the toolchain phase becomes Phase 5** (supersedes the phase numbering D1–D90 assume). A new Phase 4 measures rtoskit-libc against Arm Optimized Routines (AOR): its `string/arm` routines, its scalar `math`, and its Armv6-M `fp` soft-float, for code size, instruction counts and accuracy, with the same tools as phases 2 and 3 (`size-report`, `bench-runner`, the accuracy tests). The crosstool-NG integration is now Phase 5 (`phase-5.md`, steps P5.xx). Where a decision or research note of phases 1–3 says "Phase 4" about the toolchain, it means Phase 5. AOR is built and linked only to measure it (MIT OR Apache-2.0 WITH LLVM-exception); nothing is copied or translated from it (clean-room, as for every other library). | AOR holds the reference Arm implementations of the routines this library optimises. Measuring against them before the toolchain is frozen shows where the library stands and what is worth a later step. |
| D92 | **How rtoskit-libc is compared with AOR** (P4.01, `docs/research/optimized-routines.md`): only what runs on our cores is compared. `string`: `strcmp` on armv6-m/armv6s-m, and `memchr`, `memset` and `strlen` on armv7e-m, -dp and armv8-m.main; AOR's `memcpy`, `strcmp` and `strcpy` are A32 or A-profile only, and it has none for x86_64. `math`: the scalar `expf`, `exp2f`, `logf`, `log2f`, `log10f`, `powf`, `sinf`, `cosf`, `sincosf`, `erff`, `exp`, `exp2`, `exp10`, `log`, `log2`, `pow` and `erf`, on every board. `fp`: `fp/armv6-m` (fadd/fsub/fmul and the comparisons) on Armv6-M, and `fp/at32` (the full RTABI set, assembled with `-Wa,-mimplicit-it=always`) on the Thumb-2 cores. AOR is fetched with a shallow `git fetch` of the pinned commit, because GitHub archive downloads are blocked here. A script compiles the selected files with each board's `CROSS_FLAGS` into `build/<board>/aor/libaor-{string,math,fp}.a`, renaming symbols to the standard and RTABI names. `math` uses AOR's own flags at `-O3` for speed and `-Os` for size, with `-std=c23` against our headers and `WANT_ERRNO=0` (which matches `MATH_ERREXCEPT`, D45). Each comparison uses the same objects and the same `libc.a`, with the AOR archive linked before `libc.a` and libgcc and `-Wl,--allow-multiple-definition`, because libgcc bundles several helpers per member; every substituted symbol is checked in the map file. Speed is measured with the `test/bench` programs plus a new `softfloat` program, size with per-routine sizes and the `test/size` images, and accuracy on the host against mpmath. AOR's fp raises no exception flags and ignores the rounding mode, so the AOR builds are measured but never conformance-tested. | Measuring only the substituted routines inside otherwise identical programs isolates their effect. Building the files directly makes the per-core selection explicit, and keeps AOR's Linux test tooling (MPFR, `mathbench`) out of the build. |
| D93 | **Building and linking AOR for the comparison** (P4.02, amends D92; `tools/scripts/aor-build`, `make aor`). AOR is fetched into `local/aor-<rev>/`. `aor-build build` writes `build/<board>/aor/libaor-{string,math,math-size,fp}.a` and `aor.json`, which lists the symbols each archive provides. AOR's `memchr.S` and `memset.S` force `.arch armv7-a`, which makes A-profile objects that the linker refuses on M-profile; the build compiles a copy without that directive, so the assembler checks them for the board's own `-march`. qemu-arm `-cpu max` would also run A-only code, so the assembler is the only M-profile check. Linking the AOR archives first in the group is not enough: a libgcc member pulled in for another symbol takes its helpers from libgcc. `aor-build`'s `link()` therefore forces (`-u`) each AOR symbol the program contains and relinks until that set stops growing. `check_substitution()` reads the map file and fails any link in which a provided symbol is not AOR's code. `aor-build smoke` (`tools/aor/smoke.c`) runs one call per substituted routine on every board. | The map check caught libgcc's comparisons on Armv6-M, which a link-order-only design would have measured as AOR's. |
| D94 | **The speed comparison with AOR** (P4.03, `docs/perf/phase-4.md`). `test/bench/softfloat` is a new benchmark program and joins the gated set of every board, in both flavours. It measures float and double add, sub, mul, div and compare, plus `f2d`/`d2f` and the 32- and 64-bit integer conversions, over 16 operand pairs, with results folded by their bits using integer operations only. `bench-runner aor` relinks the speed build's `test/bench` objects with `libaor-string.a`, `libaor-math.a` (-O3) and `libaor-fp.a` through `aor-build`'s `link()`, verifies the substitution, and counts instructions as `make bench` does. `--record` keeps the results, with the case order, in `docs/perf/aor-<arch>.json`; `--from-record` prints the same tables. The comparison is context, not a gate. | The soft-float helpers decide most libm costs on four of the five Arm boards, and no existing case isolated them. A record that regenerates byte-identical tables keeps `docs/perf/phase-4.md` reproducible without rerunning qemu. |
| D95 | **The size comparison with AOR** (P4.04, `docs/size/phase-4.md`). `size-report aor` measures what each AOR entry point that a board's programs use adds to an empty program (`tools/aor/size-main.c`, linked alone and with the routine forced in by `-u`, through `aor-build`'s `link()`). It does this for our size flavour, our speed flavour and AOR (`libaor-math-size.a`, `-Os`), and also relinks the `test/size` programs with AOR's archives. `--record`/`--from-record` use `docs/size/aor-<arch>.json`. AOR is measured as it builds: its `strcmp-armv6m.S` and `strlen-armv6t2.S` keep EHABI unwind entries that pull libgcc's unwinder (about 3.6 KB), and the report shows that separately rather than stripping it. Stack depths are not compared, because AOR's assembly has no call-graph information. The comparison is context, not a gate; the size baselines and the budget are unchanged. | Per-routine deltas count the tables and helpers a routine really brings, which member sizes alone hide (libgcc's 64-bit conversions pulling soft double, AOR's float functions pulling soft double). |
| D96 | **The toolchain integration leaves the phase plan** (supersedes D91's Phase 5). The crosstool-NG multilib GCC integration is no longer a phase: its plan (`docs/plan/phase-5.md`, steps P5.01–P5.07, unchanged) moved to the enhancement [issue #65](https://github.com/sgstreet/rtoskit-libc/issues/65), and Phase 4 is the last phase. Where a decision, question or note says "Phase 4" (before D91) or "Phase 5" about the toolchain, it now means issue #65. | User direction. |
| D97 | **The accuracy comparison with AOR** (P4.05, `docs/research/optimized-routines.md`, `tools/scripts/aor-accuracy`). A host program calls our x86_64 functions (`libc.a` with every symbol prefixed `rt_`) and AOR's (entry points renamed `aor_`) and measures both against MPFR at 200 bits. Float functions are checked over all 2^32 arguments: every argument where the two libraries differ, and 1 in 16 where they agree. Doubles and `powf` are sampled, the three directed rounding modes are sampled, and special arguments are checked with their exception flags. The correctly rounded reference is MPFR's own result in the target format and mode, so its ternary value counts. Arm targets are sampled under qemu and measured on the host. `libmpfr-dev` is a host-only dependency of this test; nothing ships with it. The run found 8 float arguments that our functions round incorrectly, which contradicts D47's "correctly rounded". They are misrounded on every core, as P4.06 found (Q19). | Exhaustive float checks are the only way to back a correct-rounding claim; checking every disagreement plus a sample of agreements keeps a run to about 5 minutes per function. |
| D98 | **Correctly rounded float functions on the double path** (answers Q19; `docs/research/float-hard-cases.md`). On x86_64 and armv7e-m-dp every unary float function is its double function rounded to float (D45, D47). Where the exact value lies within a double ulp of a float halfway point, that second rounding can go the wrong way. An exhaustive search of the 36 transcendental unary float functions over all 2^32 arguments (`tools/scripts/float-hard-cases`, `tools/libm/float_hard_cases.c`, against MPFR) found 49 such arguments in 18 functions. In every one, the double result is exactly a float halfway point. Each function now passes its double result, its argument and a table of its hard cases (argument and correctly rounded result, 8 bytes each) to `__libc_float_round` (`internal/math.h`). An inline test on the double's low 29 bits sends only a result within 256 double ulps of a halfway point to the shared out-of-line lookup `__libc_float_case`. That lookup nudges a matching case's result toward the exact value, whose side the double result gives, so every rounding direction and inexact come out right. Double rounding cannot go wrong in a directed mode, and the search confirms this. The build's `-std=c23` (no FP contraction) makes the armv7e-m-dp double results identical to x86_64's, and `test/unit/float_hard` checks the 49 cases in all four directions on both. Cost: +5 to +9 instructions per call in the 18 functions (+0.6–3.1 % on x86_64, +1.1–5.9 % on armv7e-m-dp; new benchmark baselines), and +352 bytes in armv7e-m-dp `math-float` (64 bytes left in its size budget). The soft-float cores do not use these wrappers: their float-float layer (D57) has its own documented allowance (Q26). The binary float functions (`powf`, `atan2f`, `hypotf` and the like) cannot be searched exhaustively and remain sampled. | Exhaustive search is the only way to back a correct-rounding claim for float, and once the hard cases are known a lookup gated by an integer test costs far less than a more precise evaluation of every argument. |
| D99 | **Our own double-precision helpers on the single-precision FPUs** (answers Q24 for armv7e-m and armv8-m.main; `docs/research/softdouble.md`). A new libm layer, `soft-dp` (D46), on both cores, replaces every libgcc helper the compiler calls for double arithmetic and conversions; no libgcc floating-point member is linked any more. Only round to nearest, no flags (D44). The hot helpers are Thumb-2 assembly, complete with zeros, subnormals, infinities and NaN: `__aeabi_dadd`/`__aeabi_dsub` (operands ordered by magnitude, b shifted into three words with a sticky bit, a one-place or CLZ normalisation, exact subnormal results), `__aeabi_dmul` (UMULL/UMLAL/UMAAL, subnormals normalised, underflow rounded once on the subnormal grid), `__aeabi_d2f`, `__aeabi_f2d`, `__aeabi_d2iz`, `__aeabi_d2uiz`, `__aeabi_l2d` and `__aeabi_ul2d`. A first all-C version was correct but twice as slow as libgcc's assembly at `-Os`. The rest is C: `__aeabi_ddiv` (base-2^18 long division, each digit estimated from a single-precision reciprocal and corrected by the exact remainder), the comparisons (one shared ordering; `dcmpun` tests NaN directly because every `isnan` calls it), `i2d`/`ui2d`, the truncating 64-bit conversions, and `d2f` outside float's normal range. The helpers use the base calling convention (`pcs("aapcs")`, RTABI 4.1.1) and are weak, so a libgcc member that libgcc's own complex helpers pull in overrides them instead of clashing. Out-of-range float-to-integer conversions saturate as `VCVT` does. The step found a defect in libgcc's `__aeabi_dadd`/`dsub` on both cores: an effective subtraction across more than one binade whose result drops a binade is off by one ulp (4 of 60,000 test cases). `test/unit/softdp` hashes 30,000 cases of every operation on every board against the value x86_64's hardware fixes; it checks the layer's C and the C models of the assembly (`reference_add.c`, `reference_multiply.c`) against the hardware on x86_64, and the assembly against the models on the soft-dp cores. Speed (instructions per operation, both flavours, both cores): `dadd` 63.9 → 55.2, `dsub` 67.3 → 58.1, `dmul` 57.6 → 54.0, `ddiv` 583 → 174, `dcmp` 48.9 → 43.9, `i2d` 40.9 → 21.6, `l2d` 48.6 → 30.1, `d2l` 178 → 44.9, `f2l` 141 → 27.4; the double libm functions 15 % fewer instructions on the geometric mean (up to 47 %, `cbrt`; `sqrt` and `fmod` +0.1–0.3 %, within noise), `strtod` up to 30 % (release) and 51 % (speed) fewer, `sscanf("%lf")` 13–18 % fewer. Accepted: `fmod` latency +4.3 % (the benchmark's `x + y * 0.0` dependency costs about one ordinary addition through the zero paths, where libgcc's are shorter; `fmod` itself and its throughput are unchanged). Size: `math-mix` −156 bytes and `hello-printf-float` −668 (armv7e-m), −156 and −660 (armv8-m.main). | A correctness defect, 2–6× slower division and 64-bit conversions, and 1.6–1.9 KB per 64-bit conversion were libgcc's; the replacement is smaller and at least as fast in every case but one benchmark artefact. |
| D100 | **The soft-dp helpers are strong, and the layer defines libgcc's callers of them** (supersedes D99's weak definitions). Weak helpers turned a clash with libgcc into a silent substitution. libgcc's `__muldc3`, `__divdc3`, `__divsc3` and `__powidf2` call the double helpers, and GNU ld resolves those calls while it scans libgcc. So a complex multiplication or `__builtin_powi` linked libgcc's double objects (its misrounding addition included) beside ours, and a complex division with strong helpers failed with a duplicate `__aeabi_dcmpgt`. Every helper is now a strong global symbol, and the `soft-dp` layer defines those four from C23 Annex G.5.2. Multiplication recovers infinite results when both parts are NaN; double division scales the divisor by `logb`/`scalbn` and recovers the infinity and zero cases; float division is computed in double; `powi` squares. `__mulsc3` stays libgcc's (single-precision FPU arithmetic, no double helper). `test/unit/complex_ops` checks them on every board. A program that reaches a libgcc helper the layer lacks (the fixed-point conversions, the GNU-named `__adddf3` family) now links libgcc's object or fails with a duplicate symbol, rather than silently mixing. | The user's choice: a clash should be a link error to fix, never a silent substitution of a libgcc helper known to misround. |
| D101 | **The soft-dp comparisons in Thumb-2 assembly** (amends D99; `docs/research/softdouble.md`). `__libc_softdouble_compare` is now a hidden assembly routine. It sets the flags as `CMP` would for two ordered integers: Z for equal, C clear for less, and C set with Z clear for unordered, which none of the callers' conditions reads as true. It touches only ip. Each operand costs one compare for an exponent field of 2047 before any NaN test. Same signs compare as unsigned 64-bit integers, reversed for two negatives; only opposite signs test for two zeros. `__aeabi_dcmp{lt,le,eq}` are a call and one conditional move; `gt` and `ge` exchange the operands first. `__aeabi_dcmpun` stays C. The C ordering becomes the test's model (`test/unit/softdp/reference_compare.c`), checked against the hardware on x86_64; the soft-dp cores check all six predicates against it. `dcmp` 43.9 → 28.9 instructions (libgcc 48.9, AOR 20.2). The double libm is 0.8–2.2 % faster on 56 cases, both flavours, both cores, with nothing slower. Size: `math-mix` +24 bytes, the operand exchange in `gt` and `ge`. | The comparisons run several times in every libm call; the shared flag-setting routine keeps the size near the C version's. |
| D102 | **No libgcc object may replace one of the library's symbols, and `make check` proves it** (amends D85, D99, D100; `docs/research/softdouble.md`). `tools/scripts/libgcc-check` runs last in `make check`: it reads every link map of the configuration and fails when a program includes a libgcc member that defines a global symbol some `libc*.a` also defines. Strong symbols (D100) only turn a clash into an error when both definitions are linked; a libgcc helper whose callee only libgcc defines is resolved inside libgcc while the linker scans it, and can bring a libgcc copy of one of our helpers in first. The first run found exactly that on Armv6-M (`bench-stdio`, `bench-strto`): libgcc's `__aeabi_d2lz` (`_fixdfdi`) calls `__fixunsdfdi`, which calls `__fixunsdfsi`, which subtracts in floating point, so libgcc's `subdf3.o` (1444 bytes, its own `__aeabi_dadd`) replaced D85's. A new libm layer, `soft-int`, shared by the nofp and soft-dp cores, holds the conversions to 64-bit integers in C (`__aeabi_d2lz`, `d2ulz`, `f2lz`, `f2ulz`, moved from soft-dp); the nofp layer adds `__aeabi_d2uiz` and `__aeabi_f2uiz`. `softdouble.h` moves to `libc/internal/`. After the change no program in the 18 configurations links such a member. Armv6-M: `libc.a` +618 bytes, no reference image changes; `d2l` 696 → 62.5 and `f2l` 689 → 45.4 instructions (the other faster cases are the benchmarks' own conversions). armv7e-m and armv8-m.main are unchanged. The long comparison of the soft-dp layer is now reproducible: `softdp.elf [iterations [float-stride]]` and `tools/scripts/softdp-diff`, which by default runs 100 million cases and every float through `f2d` (x86_64 against the hardware; armv7e-m and armv8-m.main assembly against the models under qemu). Its first run passed with no difference: x86_64 in 2 minutes, armv7e-m and armv8-m.main in 28 minutes each. The archives collect their members by wildcard from the build tree, so a source moved out of a module leaves its old object in `libc.a` until `make clean`; a fresh checkout is unaffected. | D100 made a clash an error only when both copies are linked; a check over the link maps catches the substitution that strong symbols cannot, and the long runs that D99 and D101 cite should be repeatable. |
| D103 | **The heap stays `__rtos_heap_region`; an `sbrk`-backed RTOS takes region 0 from its break** (confirms D31; `docs/research/malloc.md`, "Growing with sbrk, evaluated"). All three allocators were rebuilt to grow on demand through an `__rtos_sbrk` seam (whole-stride growth, exact in place, used bridge chunks over memory another `sbrk` user took, requests bounded by `PTRDIFF_MAX / 4`, a static TLSF control block). It passed every check and mutant, and after tuning was 0.5–3.3 % faster on Arm, but it cost 68–76 bytes in each malloc reference image and 12 bytes of RAM, gave up the last large allocations of a fixed heap (or 40–50 bytes more to avoid that), made allocation call into the port at any time, and moved disjoint-memory handling into the port. By the user's choice the region seam stays, and a port whose RTOS owns the heap through `sbrk` (pi-pico's `sys/sbrk.c`) implements `__rtos_heap_region` by taking one block from that `sbrk` at index 0, at the first allocation: everything up to the heap's end, or all but a reserve for the RTOS's other `sbrk` users, which keep working above it. The `sbrk` implementation is kept on the branch `claude/sbrk-allocators` (fa6bbe1). | The sharing an `sbrk` heap needs is met at the port with no libc change, and the regions stay smaller, deterministic and able to use the whole heap. |
| D104 | **`.preinit_array` belongs to the port** (amends D10; `docs/research/tls-and-startup.md`, `include/rtos/rtos-port.h`). `__libc_start_main` runs only `.init_array`, then `main` and `exit`. The port's startup runs `.preinit_array` (bounds `__preinit_array_start`/`__preinit_array_end` from the default linker scripts, declared in `rtos-port.h`) once the initial thread's TLS is in place and before calling `__libc_start_main`. Neither C nor the compilers require the table: C23 only asks that static objects be initialised before `main`, and GCC and Clang put constructors in `.init_array`; `.preinit_array` is an ELF gABI convention that newlib, picolibc, glibc and musl honour at startup. Both ports run it, so code that follows the convention is unaffected. `test/unit/startup` checks the order (preinit hook, constructor, `main`, each once, `errno` usable in the hook) through the Linux port, and `test/unit/none` checks that `__rtos_none_start` runs it once with the TLS in place; both catch a missing run and a double run. | An RTOS can keep early hooks there and walk them itself (pi-pico's `PREINIT_*` tiers run from its own `_start`, before the scheduler); if libc also ran the table, every hook would run twice. |
| D105 | **`make library`, and building from another project** (a first slice of D16 and issue #65; `docs/configuration.md`, "Building from another project"). The new goal builds and installs only the headers, the `libc*.a` archives and the port archives, with the full build's ordering (toolchain, then `libc` and `port`), at every level (all boards, all build types, or one of each). A parent make runs it as a sub-make and passes `PROJECT_ROOT`, `TOOLS_ROOT`, `OUTPUT_ROOT`, `PREFIX`, `BOARD_TYPE` and `BUILD_TYPE` on the command line: the top-level `Makefile` takes those six from the environment when set, and a parent with the same conventions (pi-pico exports all six) would otherwise hand over its own; everything else is assigned with `:=` and cannot leak in. A `CROSS_COMPILE` given on the command line or in the environment is used as it is: setup downloads no compiler, and stops if `${CROSS_COMPILE}gcc` is missing or older than GCC 14 (D9). A pi-pico-style parent wraps the sub-make in its own module glue, `rtoskit-libc.mk` at the root of the checkout (found by the parent's one-level module search, never read by this project's build), which empties `SUBDIRS` so the parent does not walk this tree, maps the parent's core to a board, and gives the parent fixed `include`/`lib` paths; by the user's choice that file belongs to the parent and is only described here. Verified from a scratch parent that exports pi-pico-style values for every shared variable: with the overrides it builds only `libc/` and `port/` and installs 31 headers and seven archives; without them the sub-make fails to find `tools/makefiles/tree.mk`. In a clean checkout, an external `CROSS_COMPILE` builds the library without creating `local/`, a missing compiler and a GCC 13 stand-in stop with an error, and no `CROSS_COMPILE` still downloads as before. Still open under issue #65: building by `ARCH_CROSS` without a board file, a sysroot layout with `MULTIDIR`, and `install-headers`. | A parent build needs the library without the test suite, with its own compiler, and needs to know which inherited variables would break it; this is usable now and does not foreclose D16's interface. |
| D106 | **The build loops over architectures, not boards** (supersedes the board files of D22 and D36; amends D105). `ARCHS ?= x86_64 armv6-m armv6s-m armv7e-m armv7e-m-dp armv8-m.main`, and `ARCH_CROSS` (default `all`) selects one, naming its toolchain file directly; output goes to `build/<arch>/<build-type>/` and `images/<arch>/<build-type>/`. The six `linux-<arch>.mk` board files are gone: they only chose `ARCH_CROSS` and the test settings. Programs the build links (tests, examples, size and benchmark programs) bind to the Linux test port (`RTOS_PORT := linux` in `common.mk`), whose per-family file `tools/makefiles/port-linux-<family>.mk` sets `TARGET_RUNNER` (`qemu-arm -cpu max` on Arm) and appends `port/linux/arm/headers.ld` to the Arm link flags (`BOARD_LDFLAGS` is gone); `LIBC_MALLOC ?= nano` moves to `common.mk`. The libc-test baselines are `test/libc-test/expected/<arch>.txt` (renamed, unchanged); `size-report`, `bench-runner`, `aor-build`, `aor-accuracy` and `softdp-diff` take `--arch` and read `build/<arch>/<type>`, and the size, perf and AOR records drop their `board` field. For a parent build (D105), `ARCH_CROSS` replaces `BOARD_TYPE` among the six variables to pass on the sub-make's command line; it now matters more, because pi-pico exports its own `ARCH_CROSS` (a CPU name), and the glue maps the parent's core to an architecture (`cortex-m33` to `armv8-m.main`) instead of a board. An `ARCH_CROSS` without a toolchain file (one a parent exported) stops at once with the list of architectures, instead of compiling with the host's defaults. Validated from a fresh tree: `make check` passes in all 18 configurations with the same unit-test counts as before, libc-test 219 passed and no regressions, libgcc-check clean; `size-report diff` shows no image change and both budgets hold; `make bench` finds no case changed on x86_64 and armv6-m (the code is identical); the AOR smoke test passes on armv7e-m and `softdp-diff` on all three architectures through the renamed scripts; a pi-pico-style parent that exports `ARCH_CROSS=cortex-m33` builds `library` with the override and is stopped without it. | The boards existed only to pick an architecture and the test port; looping over architectures makes `ARCH_CROSS` the one selector, as D16 and issue #65 need for multilib builds, and a parent maps its core straight to it. |
| D107 | **The host compiler is any GCC 14 or newer, found rather than named** (amends D9's tooling; `tools/scripts/host-cc`). `tools/makefiles/x86_64.mk` named `gcc-14` because Ubuntu 24.04's `gcc` is 13, so a machine whose `gcc` is 14 or newer but has no `gcc-14` could not build. `tools/scripts/host-cc` now prints the compiler: `HOST_CC` when set (checked: it must run and be GCC 14 or newer), else plain `gcc` when it is new enough, else the newest `gcc-NN` from 20 down to 14; it exits 1 with a message when there is none. `x86_64.mk` runs it once and exports the result with a marker (`HOST_CC_CHECKED`), so the sub-makes inherit it instead of checking again (one `-dumpversion` per build); without a compiler it stops with a message that names `HOST_CC` and building the Arm architectures alone. `aor-build`, `aor-accuracy` and `float-hard-cases` use the same script. Validated: a clean x86_64 `make check` in all three build types with no size change; a `gcc` that is GCC 14 builds and passes with no `gcc-14` in any command; `HOST_CC` set to GCC 13 on the command line or in the environment stops with the message. | The requirement is a C23 compiler, not a package name; naming one version broke clean builds on any other distribution. |
| D108 | **An `armv7-m` architecture: Thumb-2 without an FPU or the DSP extension** (Cortex-M3, and a Cortex-M4 or M33 built without its FPU; `docs/research/armv7-m.md`). `tools/makefiles/armv7-m.mk` builds with `-mthumb -march=armv7-m -mfloat-abi=soft` (GCC's `thumb/v7-m/nofp` multilib), soft `<fenv.h>`, `LIBC_FPU := nofp`; such a core could only link `armv6s-m` (Thumb-1) before. Armv7-M's libgcc groups its soft-float helpers per object (`fadd` with `fsub` and the integer-to-float conversions, `dadd` with `dsub`, ...), unlike Armv6-M's one function per object, so a library helper that calls into a group clashes with the group's own copy. Hence: (1) the six Armv6-M helpers (`__aeabi_fsub fmul fdiv f2uiz dsub d2uiz`, D55, D85) move from `nofp` to a new layer `nofp-thumb1`, which only armv6-m and armv6s-m list, first, so their archive order and images stay byte for byte as before; `nofp` keeps the integer `sqrtf` and the fixed-point speed paths, which armv7-m lists too; armv7-m takes every float helper from libgcc's Thumb-2 assembly. (2) armv7-m uses `soft-dp` for doubles, as the FPU cores do (D99, D100), but its `__aeabi_dmul.S` uses `UMAAL`, a DSP instruction, so the new layer `soft-dp-nodsp` holds the same routine with each `UMAAL` written with `UMULL`/`UMLAL` and an add with carry (three instructions more), and `soft-dp`'s new `subdir.mk` drops a file a later layer replaces. (3) `libc/string/thumb2/strchr.S` (`UADD8`/`SEL`) moves to `libc/string/thumb2-dsp`; the DSP cores list `thumb2-dsp thumb2 thumb1` in the speed flavour, armv7-m `thumb2 thumb1` (its `strchr` is the C one). (4) `libc/setjmp/armv7-m` saves r4-r11, sp and lr, the FPU cores' layout without the VFP registers. armv7-m joins `ARCHS` and the scripts' lists (`size-report`, `bench-runner`, `aor-build`, `softdp-diff`); `make aor` builds AOR's math and fp for it, not AOR's string routines, which need DSP. Validated: `make check` passes in all 21 configurations (armv7-m: 50 unit tests, libc-test 219 passed, recorded as `expected/armv7-m.txt`), libgcc-check clean everywhere; `size-report diff` shows no change on the six other architectures; `softdp-diff` passes on armv7-m (100 million cases and the float sweep); AOR's smoke test passes. Against armv6s-m on the same core, armv7-m's images are 5–15 % smaller (`docs/size/armv7-m.md`), and it takes 0.36–0.53 of the instructions in strto, malloc, math and softfloat, 0.69–0.73 in stdio, 0.80 in the speed flavour's string programs (`docs/perf/armv7-m.md`). New size and benchmark baselines, and an armv7-m entry in `docs/size/budget.json` (the other architectures' budgets unchanged). | A Thumb-2 core without an FPU was served only by Thumb-1 code; the file-selecting layers let it take the Thumb-2 C code, libgcc's Thumb-2 helpers and the integer double helpers without any `#if`, and without changing a byte of the other architectures. |
| D109 | **`soft-sp`: the library's own float run-time ABI helpers on armv7-m** (amends D108's "every float helper from libgcc"; `docs/research/softfloat-thumb2.md`). A new layer `libc/math/soft-sp`, listed on armv7-m only (`sp sp-nearest nofp soft-sp soft-dp soft-dp-nodsp soft-int`), defines in Thumb-2 assembly `__aeabi_fadd fsub frsub fmul fdiv`, `__aeabi_cfcmpeq cfcmple cfrcmple` and `fcmpeq fcmplt fcmple fcmpge fcmpgt` (one hidden `__libc_softfloat_compare` that changes only ip and the flags, as RTABI 4.1.2 requires), and `__aeabi_i2f ui2f l2f ul2f`; in C (Annex G.5.2) `__mulsc3` and `__powisf2`. These are every symbol of libgcc's three grouped objects `_arm_addsubsf3.o`, `_arm_muldivsf3.o` and `_arm_cmpsf2.o`, plus libgcc's callers of them, so no libgcc object defines a library symbol (D100, D102); `f2iz`, `f2uiz`, `fneg` and `fcmpun` stay libgcc's single-symbol objects. Round to nearest, ties to even, subnormals throughout, no flags (D44); a NaN operand gives itself quietened, an invalid operation the default NaN. `fadd` orders by magnitude, adds or subtracts both-normal operands with the hidden bit at bit 31 and a sticky bit, and keeps a general path for a subnormal operand; `fmul` is one `UMULL`; `fdiv` four `UDIV`/`MLS` 8-bit digit steps. A new `test/unit/softsp` runs on every Arm core against whatever provides the helpers (`soft-sp`, libgcc, `nofp-thumb1`): random and special cases per operation with the double operation rounded to float as the reference (double rounding is innocuous for + − × ÷ since 53 ≥ 2·24 + 2), an integer model for `l2f`/`ul2f`, and inline assembly that checks the `cfcmp` flags and that r0–r3 survive; it caught each of eight planted bugs. Results on armv7-m (instructions per operation, libgcc → `soft-sp`): `fadd` 59.3 → 56.3, `fsub` 58.2 → 52.1, `fmul` 39.9 → 39.3, `fdiv` 156 → 57.7, `fcmp` 42.9 → 28.9, `i2f` 29.9 → 20.9, `l2f` 32.9 → 26.9, and `ddiv` 354 → 245 since `soft-dp` takes its reciprocal with a float division; math geomean 0.938 (size flavour) and 0.947 (speed), no case slower except `strtof/exp` (+0.1 %, +0.9 % in the speed flavour: libgcc's `fmul` short-cuts a power-of-two operand, which would cost every other product a test). Images: `hello-printf-float` −824 bytes, `float-exact` −184, `math-float` −108, `math-mix` −112, the integer programs unchanged; `libc.a` +2252 bytes for the twenty new members (`docs/size/armv7-m.md`, `docs/perf/armv7-m.md`). Validated: `make check` passes in all 21 configurations (libc-test 219 everywhere, `softsp` on every Arm core in every build type), libgcc-check clean on every link map, `size-report diff` unchanged on the six other architectures, the size budget holds; armv7-m's speed-flavour `math-float` was already over its budget at D108 (10644 bytes against 9960) and is now 10536 (Q28). On Armv6-M, libgcc's Thumb-1 `cfcmp` forms do not give RTABI's flags (GCC never calls them there), so the test checks them only where they conform; and `cfrcmple` may return r0 and r1 swapped, as libgcc, compiler-rt and FreeBSD all do. | Every float operation on armv7-m was a call into libgcc's grouped Thumb-2 helpers, whose division and comparisons took two to three times the instructions Thumb-2 needs; owning the whole group is the only way to replace any of it (D100), and it shrinks every float image since a program now links only the helpers it calls. |
| D110 | **armv7-m's speed-flavour budget is locked from its speed images** (resolves Q28; amends D71's speed margin and D87 for armv7-m only). D108 locked armv7-m's `docs/size/budget.json` entry from the size flavour, and the speed flavour's `math-float` (10536 bytes) exceeded it with D87's 20 % (9960): the speed flavour builds `nofp/speed`'s fixed-point `expf exp2f logf log2f sinf cosf tanf erff erfcf` (D86–D88), which do not exist in the size flavour. They are kept: on armv7-m they take 0.03–0.27 of the size flavour's instructions (`expf` 5431 → 280, `sinf` 9154 → 2499, `erff` 66112 → 2079), the same ratios as on armv6-m, since their integer arithmetic suits `UMULL` and the soft-float helpers they avoid are still calls (D109 made each one cheaper, not free). `budget.json` gains `flavour_images`: figures locked from a flavour's own build for named architectures, which `size-report budget --flavour F` holds that architecture to with no margin, in place of the size figures plus the flavour's margin. `size-report --build-type speed budget --flavour speed --lock --arch armv7-m` wrote armv7-m's entry (every reference program; `math-float` 10536, `math-mix` 32400); `--lock` with a flavour other than size requires `--arch` and keeps the rest of the file. The other architectures' budgets and margins are unchanged; both flavours' budget checks pass on every architecture. | A budget that the speed flavour cannot meet without giving up a 4–30× gain in the functions it exists to speed up is not a useful limit; locking armv7-m's speed images keeps a growth gate on them, stricter than a margin, while the size flavour's budget stays as it was. |
| D111 | **A floor under the speed margin** (resolves Q16; amends D71/D73's +10 % and D87's per-program margins for the speed flavour; P5.01, `docs/plan/phase-5.md`). A speed image's code may exceed its locked size figure by the larger of its margin and 256 bytes; RAM keeps the percentage, and architectures with their own speed figures (D110) keep no margin. `budget.json` gains `floor_bytes` (`speed`: 256, `size`: 0) and `size-report budget` applies it. Headroom of the small images rises from 25–37 to 96–144 bytes (`malloc-heavy`, `malloc-heavy-tiny`, every core); the larger images, where 10 % already exceeds 256 bytes, are unchanged (`hello-printf-int-nofloat` 97–101). The size flavour's budget is unchanged. Phase 5 schedules the work this and the later budget decisions unblock: Thumb-2 `strlen` (P5.02), `memchr` (P5.03, Q21), fixed-point `powf`/`log10f` (P5.04, Q25). | A speed kernel is shared library code that costs the same bytes in every image that links it, so a percentage of a 2 KB image (about 200 bytes) blocked kernels that the large images could take easily; a floor fixes the small images without loosening the large ones. |
| D112 | **A Thumb-2 `strlen` in the speed flavour** (P5.02, `docs/research/strlen-thumb2.md`; amends D74, which kept the Thumb-1 `strlen` for the `malloc-heavy` budget, Q16, and D110's armv7-m figures). `libc/string/thumb2/strlen.S`: bytes to a word boundary, one word to an 8-byte boundary, then two words per `LDRD`, each with the classic zero-byte test and its own exit (`RBIT`/`CLZ`); 4.5 instructions a word, nothing pushed, no DSP, so armv7-m takes it too. Aligned to 8, every read stays inside one 32-byte MPU granule. A first version walked bytes to the 8-byte boundary and was 4–12 % slower for short strings at offset 1; the word step removed every regression. 108 bytes against Thumb-1's 44. On every Thumb-2 core, instructions per call: 1 byte 42 → 35, 7 bytes 61 → 36, 1024 bytes 1315 → 1187, `mix/strlen` 113 → 103; 119 cases faster, none slower. The images that link `strlen` grow by 64 bytes, inside D111's floor on armv7e-m, armv7e-m-dp and armv8-m.main; armv7-m's speed figures (D110) are re-locked with it (`hello-puts` 2051, `malloc-heavy` 2138, `malloc-heavy-tiny` 2078). The size flavour is unchanged. Validated: `make check` passes in all 21 configurations (libc-test 219, `string_kernels` on every core and flavour), libgcc-check clean, `make bench` recorded on the four Thumb-2 cores with no case slower, `size-report diff` unchanged (size flavour), both budgets pass. | P3.02b measured this kernel and dropped it only for the budget; D111's floor makes room on the FPU cores, and on armv7-m the same 64 bytes buy the same gain, which is what D110's exact lock asks a decision for. |
| D113 | **A Thumb-2 `memchr` in the speed flavour** (P5.03, resolves Q21; `docs/research/memchr-thumb2.md`; amends D110/D112's armv7-m figures). `libc/string/thumb2/memchr.S`: c replicated into r1; bytes to a word boundary; two aligned words per iteration, each XORed with the pattern and given the classic zero-byte test with its own exit (`RBIT`/`CLZ`); one more word; bytes. No DSP (every Thumb-2 core takes it), nothing pushed; the byte loops compare against r1's top byte. Only aligned words wholly inside the n bytes are read, each inside one 32-byte granule, so a read past a match, where n may exceed the object (7.26.5.2p2), never leaves the granule holding it. An `LDRD` version (6.5 instructions a word) was 188 bytes and put `hello-puts` and `hello-printf-int-nofloat` 3–12 bytes over the speed budget on the FPU cores, both of which link `memchr` (line-buffered writes); the two-`LDR` loop (7 a word) is 154 bytes against the C's 88. Instructions per call: 1024 bytes 2615 → 1821, 16 bytes 95 → 57, 0 bytes 29 → 24; 49 cases faster, none slower. Images that print grow by 66 bytes, inside D111's floor on armv7e-m, armv7e-m-dp and armv8-m.main; armv7-m's speed figures are re-locked (`hello-puts` 2119, `hello-printf-int-nofloat` 4865, `hello-printf-int` 7785, `hello-printf-float` 9618, `hello-printf-int-minimal` 4439). `test/unit/string_kernels` gains `memchr` (every offset, length and position, absent, aliasing bytes, c beyond a byte, n beyond the object at a page end). Also found: the build archives every `.o` present in a module's build directory, so an object whose source a kernel now shadows stays in an incrementally built `libc.a` (the generic `memchr.o`, and P5.02's Thumb-1 `strlen.o` in the speed trees); clean builds are unaffected, the stale objects were removed before measuring, and the counts confirm P5.02's figures. The size flavour is unchanged. Validated: `make check` passes in all 21 configurations (libc-test 219, `string_kernels` with the new `memchr` cases on every core and flavour), libgcc-check clean, `make bench` recorded on the four Thumb-2 cores with no case slower, `size-report diff` unchanged on every architecture (size flavour), both budgets pass. | Q21's gap was blocked only by the budget; with D111's floor a kernel that halves the word loop's cost fits once it avoids pushing registers, and on armv7-m the same bytes buy the same gain. |
| D114 | **Fixed-point `log10f` on the cores without an FPU** (P5.04a, part of Q25; `docs/research/libm-fixed.md`, P5.04a; extends D86). `libc/math/nofp/speed/log10f.c`: the D86 `log2f` core's normalised result times log10 2 to 64 bits (0x9a209a84fbcff799 · 2^-65), with `__libc_fixed_round` and the logarithm bound (8 units of 2^-32), falling back to the float-float `log10f` (D58). Run on the host over every positive finite float against `log10l`: largest error 5.38 units, 3.32 % fall back, none wrong; without the test 3,334 would be confidently misrounded. Twelve of those are in `test/unit/libm_dense/near.inc` (a mutant with the bound at 0 fails all twelve), and `libm_dense` sweeps `log10f`; a thirteenth, which the float-float `log10f` misses too (D57's allowance), is left out. The P5.03 `strlen`/`memchr` lesson applied once more: the stale `sp-nearest/log10f.o` in an incremental speed tree had been linked instead of the new file until it was removed (a separate task tracks the build fix). 488 bytes against 324; no reference image links `log10f`, so the budget is unchanged. armv6-m, armv6s-m, armv7-m speed flavour; the size flavour and the other cores are unchanged. Instructions per call (throughput): armv6-m and armv6s-m 12,365 → 678, armv7-m 6,181 → 466. Validated: `make check` passes in all 21 configurations (libc-test 219, `libm_dense` with the new sweeps and near cases), libgcc-check clean, `make bench` recorded on the three cores (2 cases faster, none slower), `size-report diff` unchanged everywhere, both budgets pass. | `log10f` took 4–4.5 times AOR's instructions on these cores (P4.03); the `logf` path with another constant closes that for 164 bytes of a function no budgeted image links. |
| D115 | **Fixed-point `powf` on the cores without an FPU** (P5.04b, part of Q25; `docs/research/libm-fixed.md`, P5.04b; extends D86). The D86 log2 core (about 2^-29 relative) is too coarse for z = y log2 |x|, which the exp2 core needs to about 2^-37 for |z| < 2^7, so `__libc_log2f_wide` evaluates log(1 + r)/(r ln 2) to degree 7 on the D86 reduction (its tables moved, unchanged, to `__libc_log2f_table.c` and shared), the deep Horner steps in Q31 and the last three in Q62 with 1/ln 2 folded into the coefficients: largest relative error 0.040 · 2^-44 over every positive float (host, against `log2l`). `libc/math/nofp/speed/powf.c` takes finite non-zero x other than ±1 and finite non-zero y (a negative x with an integer y, an odd one giving the sign) through z = y's significand times the 64-bit logarithm in units of 2^-37, the D86 exp2 core and `__libc_fixed_round` with the exp2 bound (6 units), declining for |z| ≥ 2^7, a non-normal result exponent or a rounding to infinity; everything else is the float-float `powf` (D59) unchanged. Host runs against `powl` over 658 million fast-path results in four structured sets (any binade with y = t / log2 x, x near 1, integer y, random y): largest error 2.70 units (the exp2 core's own), 0.65–2.54 % fall back, none wrong. Twelve results that are confidently misrounded without the test are in `test/unit/libm_dense/near2.inc` (a new two-argument table; a zero-bound mutant fails all twelve). Measured and not taken: `logf`/`log2f`/`log10f` on the wide core would drop the D86 core (516 bytes of `math-float`) but slow `log2f` by 73 % and `log10f` by 62 %. Instructions per call: armv6-m and armv6s-m 22,158 → 1,222, armv7-m 11,060 → 865. `math-float` and `math-mix` grow by about 1.1 KB (armv6-m) and 1.0 KB (armv7-m); D116 settles the budget. Validated: `make check` passes in all 21 configurations (libc-test 219, `libm_dense` with `near2`, `float_accuracy`), libgcc-check clean, `make bench` recorded on armv6-m, armv6s-m and armv7-m (2 cases faster, none slower), `size-report diff` unchanged everywhere, both budgets pass. | `powf` was the last libm gap of Q25 and is what `math-float` links; a 64-bit-accurate logarithm where it matters, and the existing exp2 core and rounding test, make it 13–18 times faster with every fast result correctly rounded. |
| D116 | **The speed budget of armv6-m and armv6s-m is locked from their speed images** (P5.04c, resolves Q25's budget question; extends D110 to the Armv6-M cores; amends D87 for them; decided by the maintainer). With D115, `math-float` (12,536 bytes) and `math-mix` exceed the size figures plus D87's 20 % (11,568) on armv6-m and armv6s-m, and armv7-m's locked figures. The maintainer chose locked speed figures over a larger math margin or dropping the fast path: `size-report --build-type speed budget --flavour speed --lock --arch armv6-m --arch armv6s-m --arch armv7-m` writes `flavour_images.speed` for all three cores without an FPU (armv6-m/armv6s-m: `math-float` 12,536, `math-mix` 37,136, `hello-puts` 2,239; armv7-m: `math-float` 11,580, `math-mix` 33,440). Those images are held to their own figures with no margin, so any later speed growth on these cores needs a decision entry, as D110 already required for armv7-m; the FPU cores keep the margins and D111's floor, and the size flavour's budget is unchanged. Validated: `make check` passes in all 21 configurations (libc-test 219, `libm_dense` with `near2`, `float_accuracy`), libgcc-check clean, `make bench` recorded on armv6-m, armv6s-m and armv7-m (2 cases faster, none slower), `size-report diff` unchanged everywhere, both budgets pass. | The fixed-point paths (D86–D88, D114, D115) are what the speed flavour exists for on these cores, and they cost far more than a percentage of the size images; exact locks keep a growth gate on every image instead. |
| D117 | **GNU make 4.4 and `.NOTINTERMEDIATE`: a missing archive is rebuilt** (amends D22's build conventions and D9's tool floor; the maintainer's decision). `tools/makefiles/project.mk` declared a blanket `.SECONDARY:`, which makes every target secondary, that is intermediate but never deleted, so make did not rebuild a missing intermediate while what was built from it was newer: deleting `build/<arch>/<type>/libc/libc.a` did nothing while `images/<arch>/<type>/lib/libc.a` was newer (P5.03, P5.04a). It is replaced by `.NOTINTERMEDIATE:` with no prerequisites (GNU make 4.4): no target is intermediate, so nothing built is deleted and a missing archive or object is rebuilt. Make 4.3 ignores the special target without a word, so the top-level `Makefile` stops unless `.FEATURES` lists `notintermediate`; the floor is GNU make 4.4 (Ubuntu 26.04 has it, 24.04 has 4.3), and the cloud session hook builds make 4.4.1 (checksum-checked) into `/usr/local` when needed. Not taken: per-module object lists (`.objects`) that would also keep a shadowed variant's stale object out of an incrementally built `libc.a`; an archive still takes every `*.o` in its children's build directories, so after adding or removing a shadowing file the stale object must be deleted or the architecture cleaned (`CLAUDE.md`, Archives). Verified with make 4.4.1 on armv7e-m's speed tree: deleting only the build tree's `libc.a` rebuilds it, and make deletes nothing; make 4.3 stops with the requirement. Validated with make 4.4.1: `make check` passes in all 21 configurations (libc-test 219, libgcc-check clean), make deleted no file, `size-report diff` is unchanged on every architecture and both budgets pass. The duplicate scan found one stale `memchr.o` in armv7e-m's speed tree, left by the shadowing test, which the documented delete removed. | `.NOTINTERMEDIATE` states the intent directly and needs one line; the maintainer prefers the newer make to a mechanism of the project's own. |
| D118 | **Phase 6: every remaining Arm architecture** (resolves Q10, [issue #90](https://github.com/sgstreet/rtoskit-libc/issues/90); `docs/plan/phase-6.md`; extends D24, D106, D108). The library is built, tested and measured on every Arm architecture GCC 16.2 targets, not only the Cortex-M cores of the target matrix. In scope: `armv8-m.base` (Cortex-M23); `armv8.1-m.main` (Cortex-M52, M55, M85: no FPU, single- and double-precision FPU, MVE integer and float); `armv7-r` (Cortex-R4, R5, R7, R8) and `armv8-r` (Cortex-R52) in AArch32; `armv7-a` (Cortex-A5 to A17) and `armv8-a` in AArch32 state; and AArch64 (`armv8-a` and the later levels that earn a variant, Cortex-R82), a new target triple, `aarch64-none-elf`, with GCC 16.2 fetched by `make setup` and a new `ARCH_FAMILY` `aarch64` (`port/linux/aarch64`, `port/none/aarch64`, `libc/tls/aarch64`). The classic cores (Armv4T, Armv5TE, Armv6) are Q29. Naming follows D24: one `ARCH_CROSS` per `-march`, and an FPU or extension variant takes a suffix as `armv7e-m-dp` does; P6.01's research fixes the names and the variant list. Every new architecture gets what the existing ones have: its `ARCH_CROSS` file, `setjmp`, `<fenv.h>` variant (D44), `LIBC_MATH_LAYERS` and `libc-fpu.h` (D46, D82), string kernels in both flavours (D69, D73), a libc-test baseline, size baselines and a budget entry (D51, D71), benchmark baselines (D72), a clean `libgcc-check` (D102), a research note, and its row in the README and `docs/configuration.md`. AArch32 cores share the `arm-none-eabi` headers (D15), so nothing ABI-visible may differ from the Cortex-M build; AArch64 has its own headers, and its `jmp_buf` (13 pointer registers plus d8-d15, 168 bytes, where the current expression gives 144) and `long double` (IEEE binary128 under AAPCS64, against D7's binary64) are Q32 and Q30. AArch32 binaries run under `qemu-arm -cpu max` (D36) and AArch64 under `qemu-aarch64`; `make setup-bench` builds both with plugins. Order: P6.01 research and naming, then `armv8-m.base`, the R profile, the AArch32 A profile, `armv8.1-m.main`, AArch64, the SIMD kernels (NEON, MVE, Advanced SIMD), the classic cores if Q29 admits them, and the AOR comparison on the new cores. | Users of rtoskit-libc are not limited to Cortex-M, and each architecture added so far (D108, D109) has found real gaps; one plan with a fixed per-architecture definition of done keeps the additions consistent. |
| D119 | **Continuous integration on Ubuntu 26.04** (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`; the maintainer's decision). Every pull request, when opened, reopened, marked ready or pushed to, runs one job per architecture in `ARCHS` in an `ubuntu:26.04` container: `make ARCH_CROSS=<arch> check` (all three build types: unit tests, libc-test against `test/libc-test/expected/<arch>.txt`, `libgcc-check`), and on the Arm architectures the size gates (`size-report diff` and the size and speed flavours' `budget`). Ubuntu 26.04 supplies GNU make 4.4.1 (D117, no source build), GCC 15 for the x86_64 build (D107) and qemu-user 10.2 (D36); the arm-none-eabi GCC 16.2 is the one `make setup` downloads, so the Arm size figures are the recorded ones. The x86_64 size figures depend on the host GCC and are not gated; `make bench` (a plugin qemu build, D72) is not run. A job can also be started by hand (`workflow_dispatch`). | The gates CLAUDE.md asks of every step then hold for every change, not only for the session that made it; one job per architecture keeps a run within a hosted runner's limits. |
| D120 | **The public headers compile as C++** (issue #98; `docs/research/cxx-runtime.md`; amends the conditional-compilation rule and D15's one header set per triple, which C++ shares). `include/__cdefs.h` holds the only C++ conditional most headers need: `__LIBC_BEGIN_DECLS`/`__LIBC_END_DECLS` (`extern "C"` in C++) around every header that declares a function, and `__LIBC_NULL` (`nullptr` in C++, `((void *)0)` in C) for `NULL`. The headers spell `restrict` and `typeof` as `__restrict` and `__typeof__`, which GCC and Clang (D9) take in both languages. `#ifndef __cplusplus` is allowed in a public header for what C++ lacks or the C++ standard says the header leaves out, and nothing else: the `wchar_t` typedef (a C++ keyword); the bodies of `<stdatomic.h>` and `<tgmath.h>` (C++ has `<atomic>`, `<cmath>` and `<complex>`, and libstdc++'s own versions of those headers come first on a C++ include path); the bodies of `<iso646.h>` and `<stdnoreturn.h>` (empty in C++); the `complex`, `I`, `_Complex_I` and `CMPLX*` macros of `<complex.h>` (whose declarations spell `_Complex`); the `_Generic` forms of `memchr`, `strchr`, `strpbrk`, `strrchr`, `strstr`, `bsearch` and `iseqsig`; the `<stdbit.h>` type-generic macros (`__builtin_stdc_*` are C-only; C++ calls the typed functions); and the `<inttypes.h>` self-check. `rename`'s parameters are `old_name` and `new_name` (`new` is a keyword). `test/unit/cxx/headers17.cpp` includes every header as C++17 and `cxx.cpp` as C++23 (`-Wall -Wextra -Werror`), and links functions from them against `libc.a`, which proves C linkage. | C++ programs include the C headers directly and through libstdc++'s `<c*>` wrappers; a GNU spelling where both compilers take it costs nothing, and the few conditionals that remain are the ones the C++ standard itself describes. |
| D121 | **The C++ ABI termination hooks are the C library's** (issue #98; `docs/research/cxx-runtime.md`; extends D33 and D43). `__cxa_atexit`, `__cxa_finalize`, `__dso_handle` (Itanium C++ ABI 3.3.5; Arm's `__aeabi_atexit` in libsupc++ calls `__cxa_atexit`) and `__cxa_thread_atexit` live in `libc/stdlib`, one per file. `atexit` keeps its 32-slot table of function pointers, so a C program pays nothing. `__cxa_atexit` keeps `{func, arg, dso, level}` entries in blocks of 32 (`__LIBC_CXA_BLOCK_ENTRIES`), the first static and the rest from `calloc`, so a C++ program may register any number of destructors. A run of consecutive registrations puts one internal function in the `atexit` table and each entry records that slot as its level; when the exit runner pops the slot, that function runs the newest entries of its level, newest first, so static destructors and `atexit` handlers run in one reverse order of registration (C23 7.24.4.4p3, C++ [basic.start.term]). `__cxa_finalize(d)` runs `d`'s entries once each, leaving holes the exit path skips; `__cxa_finalize(nullptr)` runs the whole `atexit` table, as the ABI says. `__dso_handle` is used only when `crtbegin.o` does not define it. `__cxa_thread_atexit` keeps a `thread_local` list of `malloc`'d nodes; `thrd_exit` runs it before the `tss` destructors, and the `atexit` runner runs it first, so the exiting thread's `thread_local` objects die before any static object; both calls are weak references, so C programs link neither. With this function in libc, libstdc++ configured against rtoskit-libc defines none of its own (`_GLIBCXX_HAVE___CXA_THREAD_ATEXIT`). The C++ runtime itself (libsupc++, the unwinder in libgcc) stays the compiler's; the thread model, `gthr-rtoskit.h`, belongs to the toolchain (Q8, rtoskit-gcc#2). `test/unit/cxx` (Arm only; the x86_64 build has no C++ runtime to link) runs static constructors and destructors interleaved with `atexit`, 70 registrations, `__cxa_finalize`, `thread_local` destructors on the main thread and a `<threads.h>` thread, exceptions through frames with destructors, `dynamic_cast` and `new[]`, linked against the downloaded toolchain's libsupc++ and libstdc++ (which `eh_alloc.o` needs for one helper), with `libc.a` first in the group. | One table with an argument per slot (musl, newlib) would grow every C program that uses `atexit` by 128 bytes; the level trick keeps the C cost at zero and still orders everything as C++ requires. |
| D122 | **In-tree conformance tests for the header APIs** (issue #93, part of #96; `docs/research/conformance.md`, "Replacing libc-test's `api/*` tests"). `test/unit/api` takes over what libc-test's 14 running `api/*` tests checked beyond what `annexb` (D50) and `headers` already did, written from N3220 clause 7: which macros are integer constant expressions (as `case` labels), the type and value of every `<float.h>` characteristic for IEC 60559 binary32 and binary64 (long double binary64, D7), `<math.h>`'s types and constants, the `<fenv.h>` macro relations, the `<signal.h>` handler types and distinct numbers, `offsetof` and `max_align_t`, `I`, the result type of every `<tgmath.h>` macro for every argument type, and, run, `<iso646.h>`, `va_start`/`va_copy`, `setjmp`/`longjmp`, `signal`'s return and `assert` with and without `NDEBUG`. `api.mk` also compiles every public header alone with `-std=c23 -pedantic-errors -Werror` on every architecture, which replaces libc-test's `-pedantic-errors` gate (`-Wno-attributes`, so a compiler that does not implement a C23 standard attribute may ignore it). The `api/*` lines stay `run` in `test/libc-test/manifest.txt` until #96 removes the harness. | The checks belong to the project, written from the standard, so libc-test can go without losing any of them. |
| D123 | **In-tree conformance cases for libc-test's `functional/*` and `regression/*`** (issue #94, part of #96; `docs/research/conformance.md`, "Replacing libc-test's `functional/*` and `regression/*` tests"). For each of the 31 running tests, the research note names the existing `test/unit` module that already covers it, and `test/unit/conformance` adds what none did. **Functional gaps:** the `strtod`/`strtof` subnormal, normal and overflow midpoints, worked out with Python's `decimal` at 2,000 digits; a 40,000-digit input; `%.300f` round trips; hexadecimal ties to even; `strtol` one past the limits at the core's own width; `sscanf` scansets, widths and `%i` prefixes; `qsort` on every permutation of five bytes; `ungetc` and the position indicator; the `<tgmath.h>` float path; 400 `gmtime_r`/`timegm` round trips; 64-bit division checked through q·y + r = x; and five-digit `strftime` years. **Regressions:** each of libc-test's 12 running `regression/*` tests becomes a `regression_<bug class>` case, so the protection outlives the harness. Inputs and expected values are our own, from the standard and from independent arithmetic, never libc-test's tables. Two expectations follow documented library behaviour rather than plain C23: plain `%Y` prints the year 10009 as POSIX `%+4Y` would (`+10009`), and after `%5c` meets the end of input only the count is checked, since what it stored is unspecified. The `functional/*` and `regression/*` lines stay `run` in `test/libc-test/manifest.txt` until #96 removes the harness. | Same as D122: the regression knowledge belongs to the project, so libc-test can go without losing it. |
| D124 | **libm conformance vectors from a correctly rounded oracle** (issue #95, part of #96; `docs/research/libm-vectors.md`). `tools/libm-vectors/libm-vectors.c`, a host program built by `tools/scripts/libm-vectors` against MPFR and GMP (never part of the build or of `make check`), writes `test/unit/libm_vectors/vectors-{double,float}.inc`. Each vector holds the correctly rounded result in all four rounding directions, the offset of the exact value from the to-nearest result, the second outputs (`frexp`, `modf`, `remquo`'s sign and low three bits) and the expected exceptions. Results are computed in MPFR at the target precision and exponent range with `mpfr_check_range` and `mpfr_subnormalize`. IEEE underflow is derived from tininess before and after rounding, and marked "either" where they differ or F.10 leaves it open. The arguments are 30 special values, 96 deterministic pseudo-random ones and, for two-argument functions, 100 special and 128 random pairs. The vectors and the MPFR version that made them are committed, so the test needs no MPFR. `test/unit/libm_vectors` runs every function libc-test's 174 `math/*` tests covered, in float, double and long double (binary64, D7, sharing the double vectors), in every rounding direction the core has. The bounds are the documented contracts: below 1 ulp for double to nearest (D47, D48); correctly rounded float to nearest where `<fenv.h>` is complete (D98), else below 1 ulp (D57); exact for the exact functions in every direction; within 1 ulp (float) and 3 ulp (double) directed; invalid, divide-by-zero and overflow exactly, and a deserved underflow to nearest. None is looser than libc-test's (1.5 ulp to nearest, 3 ulp directed). The harness prints the worst error of every function above half an ulp, so the measured accuracy stays in the `make check` log. The vectors found one bug, now fixed: `__libc_softdouble_d2f` set a narrowed NaN's sign from the double's exponent bits (`regression_nan_conversion_sign`). The `math/*` lines stay `run` in `test/libc-test/manifest.txt` until #96 removes the harness. | Vectors from an oracle the project runs itself cover every function in every direction with the flags, and keep the libm gate after libc-test's tables go. |
| D125 | **The libm vectors' accuracy findings are fixed, and the harness enforces the README's claims** (resolves Q36; `docs/research/libm-vectors.md`, "What the vectors found"). `cbrt` truncates its Newton estimate t to 26 bits so that t² is exact. It splits t² = a + b, which makes t³ = a·t + b·t and the residual y − t³ exact, and takes one Halley step on that residual. That leaves below 0.5 + 2^-25 ulp with no change of rounding mode. The sign goes on before the single rounding, so upward and downward are no longer swapped for a negative x. `__libc_math_tinyf` compares `!signbit(x) != !signbit(direction)`, because `signbit` is only zero or nonzero (C23 7.12.3.6) and differs between float and double; that fixes `asinf`, `tanf`, `atanhf`, `sinhf` and `log1pf` at −FLT_MIN rounding downward.
