For 2^-1024 < \|x\| < 2^-54, `tgamma` returns 1/x − γ: the reciprocal is a double-double in round to nearest (on x · 2^64), rounded once in the caller's direction, which also fixes `tgammaf(±FLT_MIN)`. `pow` forms y·log\|x\| in round to nearest like its cores, and below 2^-60 returns 1 ± 2^-60 with the product's sign. `test/unit/libm_vectors` now holds double and long double below 0.53 ulp to nearest (D47 and D48 promise 1 ulp; the README claims 0.53) and float correctly rounded in every direction on the cores with directed modes (those are D98's correctly rounded cores). `test/unit/conformance` has `regression_directed_rounding`. The fixes add about 500 bytes to `tgamma` on the cores without fma (its double-double reciprocal) and 80 to 170 bytes to `pow`. `make bench`: `cbrt` is 2.4 % faster on x86_64 and up to 3 % slower on the soft-double cores, and `pow` is 1.6 to 2.3 % slower where double is in hardware (`docs/perf/<arch>.md`); the baselines are re-recorded. Four budget entries are re-locked to the new figures (`docs/size/<arch>.md`): the size flavour's `math-float` on armv7e-m-dp, and the speed flavour's `math-mix` on armv7-m, armv6-m and armv6s-m. | The vectors measured the README's claims; the cheapest way to keep the claims true is to fix the code and let the gate enforce them. |
| D126 | **libc-test is removed; the in-tree conformance tests are the gate** (issue #96; supersedes D3's and D27's libc-test parts, and restates §1 goal 5, §5 and the phase DoDs). With D122 (`test/unit/api`), D123 (`test/unit/conformance`) and D124 (`test/unit/libm_vectors`) covering every check of the 219 libc-test tests that passed on every architecture, the project drops the `third-party/libc-test` submodule and `.gitmodules`, `test/libc-test/` (manifest, expected results, POSIX shim, runner and the four suite modules), `tools/makefiles/libc-test.mk`, the libc-test step of `make check` and `LIBC_TEST_UPDATE`, the CI checkout's `submodules: true` and the session hook's submodule fetch. `make check` is now the unit tests followed by `libgcc-check`, and a fresh clone needs no submodule and no access to `repo.or.cz`. `port/linux` stays the test port (D3's port half stands). Wherever a phase file's DoD says "libc-test passes" or "no libc-test regressions", read "the in-tree conformance tests pass"; a new function brings its own cases (the standard's requirements, a `regression_*` case per bug fixed, and MPFR vectors for a maths function). Completed phase files and old decision entries keep their wording as history. The research notes `docs/research/conformance.md` and `docs/research/libm-vectors.md` keep the mapping from each libc-test test to its replacement. | A clean-room library is better served by tests it owns and can extend, written from the standard, than by a third-party suite's tables with a POSIX shim; removing the submodule also removes the only network fetch `make check` needed. |
| D127 | **`BITINT_MAXWIDTH` and the `_BitInt` test follow the compiler** (issue #99; amends D37 and D50, and adds an exception to the conditional-compilation rule). arm-none-eabi GCC 14 and 15 have no `_BitInt` on 32-bit Arm and predefine no `__BITINT_MAXWIDTH__`. `<limits.h>` therefore defines `BITINT_MAXWIDTH` only `#ifdef __BITINT_MAXWIDTH__`: C23 5.2.4.2.1 requires `_BitInt`, and a macro that expands to an undeclared name would silently be 0 in `#if` and a compile error elsewhere. `test/unit/headers` requires the macro (and that it is at least `ULLONG_WIDTH`) exactly where the compiler predefines `__BITINT_MAXWIDTH__`, and rejects it otherwise. `tools/scripts/annexb-audit` has a `COMPILER_FEATURE` table, so `test/unit/annexb` checks `BITINT_MAXWIDTH` only with `__BITINT_MAXWIDTH__`. `test/unit/bitint/subdir.mk` probes the compiler with `-E` for `__BITINT_MAXWIDTH__`. Without it, the module builds `bitint.skip` (the reason) instead of the ELF, and `test/unit/run-unit` reports it as `skip` and counts it apart from passes and failures. A `.skip` beside an ELF is ignored, and the skip rule deletes a stale ELF, so a tree can change compilers. With GCC 16, which `make setup` downloads and CI uses, the test is built and must pass. Checked with a GCC 16.2 wrapper that undefines `__BITINT_MAXWIDTH__` on armv7e-m: 54 passed and 1 skipped, against 55 passed with GCC 16.2. | GCC 14 and 15 are documented as supported for the Arm builds; `make check` should complete on them and say what it could not test, rather than stop at a compile error. |
| D128 | **A minimal POSIX subset, for pi-pico and a hosted libstdc++** (amends §1's "POSIX extensions out of scope"; issue #106; phase 7). The subset is the intersection of what pi-pico's sources use (a static scan of 278 files at `origin/main` `5b268a2` against picolibc's declarations) and what a hosted libstdc++ with the stdio I/O backend, the generic locale and the single thread model needs (#106). pi-pico's stdio glue will be rewritten for this library, so picolibc internals it relies on today are not part of the subset: the `struct __file` layout and its flags, newlib's `<sys/lock.h>` and `__retarget_lock_*`, `<picotls.h>`, and `sbrk`. **Headers:** `<sys/types.h>` (`off_t`, `ssize_t`, `mode_t`, `pid_t`), `<unistd.h>`, `<fcntl.h>`, `<strings.h>` and `<sched.h>`. **Macros:** the `O_*` access and creation flags (`O_RDONLY`, `O_WRONLY`, `O_RDWR`, `O_CREAT`, `O_TRUNC`, `O_APPEND`, `O_EXCL`, `O_NONBLOCK`), `STDIN_FILENO`, `STDOUT_FILENO` and `STDERR_FILENO`, the `SCHED_*` policies, and the `errno` codes pi-pico uses beyond today's set (`EBADFD`, `EFTYPE`, `EHOSTDOWN`, `ENOTUNIQ`, `ESHUTDOWN`, `ESOCKTNOSUPPORT`). The 78 `errno` names behind C++'s `std::errc` are already defined. **Library functions:** `fdopen` and `fileno` (`<stdio.h>`), `lseek` (`<unistd.h>`), `strnlen` (`<string.h>`), `strcasecmp` (`<strings.h>`), `reallocarray` (`<stdlib.h>`), and `sincos` and `sincosf` (`<math.h>`, GNU). Optional, each of which turns a libstdc++ feature on: `clock_gettime`, `nanosleep`, `sched_yield`, `isatty` and `getentropy`. **Declared only:** `open`, `close`, `read` and `write` are declared in `<fcntl.h>` and `<unistd.h>` but defined by the port, as pi-pico's VFS does today (Q38). `ioctl` and `poll` stay in pi-pico's own `<sys/ioctl.h>` and `<poll.h>`. The library's own `FILE` code reaches I/O only through the seam. **A file descriptor is the seam handle** that `__rtos_open` returns, with 0, 1 and 2 for the standard streams (Q37): `fileno` returns a stream's handle, `fdopen` wraps one in a `FILE`, and `lseek` forwards to `__rtos_seek`. **The seam is unchanged.** Every function maps onto an existing `__rtos_*` call: `__rtos_open`, `__rtos_read`, `__rtos_write`, `__rtos_close`, `__rtos_seek` for I/O; `__rtos_clock_gettime` and `__rtos_thread_sleep` for time; `__rtos_thread_yield` for `sched_yield`. **`std::stacktrace`, capture only:** libstdc++'s `<stacktrace>` (libbacktrace, built with `--enable-libstdcxx-backtrace` and linked with `-lstdc++exp`) captures frames through libgcc's `_Unwind_Backtrace`. The addresses are symbolized on the host (`addr2line`), so the target never opens its own ELF. For this the subset adds `<sys/stat.h>` (`struct stat`, `mode_t`, `S_IS*`, `fstat` over `__rtos_seek`); `O_CLOEXEC`, `F_GETFD`, `F_SETFD` and `FD_CLOEXEC` in `<fcntl.h>`; an optional unwind-tables build of the library (a build variable adding `-funwind-tables`; the default build, its baselines and its budgets stay without it); and `__exidx_start` and `__exidx_end` in the `port/none` linker script. Symbolization on the target, which needs the unstripped ELF readable through the seam and RAM for the DWARF tables, is out of scope. pi-pico's work is to implement the seam (I/O, locks, TLS, threads, the clock and the heap region) in place of today's picolibc hooks, and to drop its own `<threads.h>` (its `<pthread.h>` stays its own). Each item gets its research note, `test/unit` cases and the usual size gate; nothing in the subset may grow an image that does not use it. | pi-pico and the toolchain's hosted libstdc++ are the library's first two consumers. A named subset covers both without importing a POSIX layer, and keeps the RTOS seam as the only path to the OS. |
| D129 | **A file descriptor is the seam handle** (P7.01, `docs/research/posix-subset.md`; answers Q37; refines D128). The seam already makes `__rtos_open` return a non-negative `int` handle and reserves 0, 1 and 2 for the standard streams (P1.09), and `struct __libc_file` already stores the handle. So `fileno` returns a stream's handle, `fdopen` makes a stream for a handle through `__libc_file_mode` and `__libc_file_new`, and `lseek` calls `__rtos_seek`, with no descriptor table and no new seam call. Handles need not be small, because nothing indexes a table by them. `port/linux` (Linux descriptors), `port/none` (only 0 to 2) and pi-pico's VFS (small integers above its reserved standard streams) all comply already. `off_t` is `long long` on every architecture, matching `__rtos_seek` (D15). A library-owned descriptor table, as in newlib, was rejected: it would cost RAM in every program that uses `fdopen` or `fileno`, and duplicate the port's own table. | The seam contract already has every property a descriptor needs. |
| D130 | **The library defines `open`, `close`, `read` and `write`, and a port may replace them** (P7.01; answers Q38; amends D128's "declared only"). Each is one archive member over the seam (D6): `open` translates the `O_*` flags to `__RTOS_OPEN_*`, and each sets `errno` from a negated return and returns -1. A port or application with its own definitions (pi-pico's VFS) replaces them at link time through archive order, as D13 does: the library's member is pulled only while the symbol is still undefined. No weak definitions are added, following the library's existing practice. The library's `FILE` code calls only the seam, never these functions, so a port's `__rtos_read` must likewise call its VFS, not `read`. `ioctl` and `poll` stay the port's own. This also makes libstdc++'s default `stdio` I/O backend usable, which needs `read`, `write`, `lseek` and `fileno`; with `stdio_pure` only `fdopen` is used, but `stdio_pure` fails to link once `<sys/stat.h>` defines `S_ISREG` (its `showmanyc()` calls `fd()`, which is defined only for `stdio`), so the toolchain should use `stdio` (Q39). | Ports without a VFS (`port/linux`, `port/none`, bare-metal boards) still need the functions, and archive order lets a VFS port override them without weak symbols. |
| D131 | **The headers a C++98 program reaches parse as `gnu++98`; the POSIX subset's headers** (P7.02, issue #106 item 1; extends D120 and the conditional-compilation rule). libstdc++ compiles part of itself (`src/c++98`) as `-std=gnu++98`, and those sources reach most C headers through `<cstdio>`, `<cstring>`, `<cstdlib>`, `<cmath>`, `<clocale>` and `<cctype>`. C++98 has no `thread_local`, `nullptr`, `alignas` or `[[…]]` attributes, so: `errno` is declared `extern __thread int errno;` (GCC and Clang take `__thread` in every C and C++ mode; the definition stays `thread_local`); `__LIBC_NULL` is `__null` in C++ (the compilers' null pointer constant, valid from C++98 on); `max_align_t` and `jmp_buf` use `__attribute__((__aligned__(n)))`; and `<assert.h>`, `<setjmp.h>`, `<stdlib.h>`, `<threads.h>` and `<time.h>` spell `__attribute__((__noreturn__))`, `__attribute__((__returns_twice__))` and `__attribute__((__deprecated__))`. `<stddef.h>` declares `nullptr_t` as `typedef decltype(nullptr) nullptr_t;` only when `__cplusplus >= 201103L`, as GCC's own `<stddef.h>` does: the one C++ version test, an exception recorded here. The C23-only headers (`<stdbit.h>` with its `[[unsequenced]]`, `<stdckdint.h>`, `<threads.h>`'s types, `<stdatomic.h>`) need not parse as C++98. `test/unit/cxx/headers98.cpp` includes every header a C++98 program can reach plus the subset's, as `-std=gnu++98 -Wall -Wextra -Werror`, and links. The step also adds the subset's headers (D128–D130): `<sys/types.h>` (`size_t`, `ssize_t` as `__PTRDIFF_TYPE__`, `off_t` as `long long`, `mode_t` as `unsigned int`, `pid_t` and `clockid_t` as `int`), `<unistd.h>` (`STD*_FILENO`, `SEEK_*`, `read`, `write`, `close`, `lseek`), `<fcntl.h>` (Linux's `O_*` values, as `errno` uses Linux's (D20), and `open`), `<strings.h>` (`strcasecmp`, `strncasecmp`) and `<sched.h>` (`struct sched_param`, which pi-pico's `<pthread.h>` uses, `SCHED_OTHER`, `SCHED_FIFO`, `SCHED_RR`, `sched_yield`), and `<errno.h>`'s `ENOTUNIQ`, `EBADFD`, `ESOCKTNOSUPPORT`, `ESHUTDOWN` and `EHOSTDOWN` (Linux values) and `EFTYPE` (200, above every Linux value), with `strerror` messages (+181 bytes in `strerror.o`). Each type repeats with the same definition in every header that needs it, as `size_t` does. `_POSIX_VERSION` is not defined: the library implements a subset, not POSIX. | libstdc++'s hosted build fails on `errno` today; GNU spellings both compilers take keep the change to one version test. |
| D132 | **The ISO headers show the POSIX subset's names outside strict C, or on request** (P7.03; extends the conditional-compilation rule and D128). `fileno` and `fdopen` (`<stdio.h>`), `strnlen` (`<string.h>`), `reallocarray` (`<stdlib.h>`), `sincos` and `sincosf` (`<math.h>`), and `clockid_t`, `CLOCK_REALTIME`, `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, `CLOCK_PROCESS_CPUTIME_ID`, `CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID`, `clock_gettime`, `clock_getres` and `nanosleep` (`<time.h>`) are not reserved by C23, so a strictly conforming program may use them (7.1.3). `<__cdefs.h>` defines `__LIBC_POSIX_VISIBLE` as 0 when `__STRICT_ANSI__` (GCC's and Clang's `-std=c23`) is defined and none of `_POSIX_C_SOURCE`, `_XOPEN_SOURCE`, `_GNU_SOURCE` or `_DEFAULT_SOURCE` is, and as 1 otherwise, and each ISO header declares those names under `#if __LIBC_POSIX_VISIBLE`: one `#if` per header, a recorded exception to the conditional-compilation rule, in the class of item 3 (feature test macros). The GNU modes pi-pico builds with (`-std=gnu11`) and C++ (g++ defines `_GNU_SOURCE`) see them; the library's own files define `_POSIX_C_SOURCE` (`_GNU_SOURCE` for `sincos`). The POSIX-only headers (`<unistd.h>`, `<fcntl.h>`, `<strings.h>`, `<sched.h>`, `<sys/types.h>`) need no guard. `CLOCK_*` are the seam's time bases (`TIME_UTC`, `TIME_MONOTONIC`, `TIME_ACTIVE`, `TIME_THREAD_ACTIVE`). `getentropy` is not provided (no port has an entropy source). | glibc, newlib and picolibc behave the same way, so programs written for them build unchanged, and strict C23 keeps its namespace. |
| D133 | **Phase 8: faster sine and cosine, without CORDIC** (the P7.03 follow-up). `sincos` and `sincosf` (P7.03) call `sin` and `cos`, so they reduce the argument twice; phase 8 gives them one reduction and both polynomials. Double `sin` and `cos` on the cores without a double FPU take 6,876 (armv7e-m) to 17,710 (armv6-m) instructions per call through soft double; phase 8 computes them in 64-bit fixed point, as P3.05g does for the float functions. CORDIC was rejected: on the double-FPU cores (armv7e-m-dp, x86_64) its ~56 dependent iterations cost more than today's ~170-instruction polynomials; on the others a fixed-point polynomial is estimated at about half CORDIC's cost (≈1,000 against 1,500–2,000 instructions on armv6-m for both results); and 64-bit CORDIC accumulates about 2^-57 absolute error, which is about 0.56 ulp near 1 and loses relative accuracy near 0, so it misses the 0.53-ulp bound (D125) without wider arithmetic. The estimates are to be confirmed by P8.01. | The shared reduction is the cheap win on every core; fixed point is the large one on the soft-double cores. |
| D134 | **P7.04: the names libbacktrace needs, an unwind-tables build, and where the `std::stacktrace` test runs** (amends D128's P7.04 items). libstdc++'s configure gives libbacktrace `alloc.c` and `read.c` when `<sys/mman.h>` is absent, so the library adds only `fstat` (`<sys/stat.h>`: the full POSIX `struct stat`, `S_IS*`, `S_I*`; over `__rtos_seek`: a handle that seeks is a regular file whose size is its end, one that fails with `ESPIPE` a character device of size 0), `fcntl` with `F_GETFD`, `F_SETFD` and `FD_CLOEXEC` (accepted, with no effect: nothing executes another program; a handle the port rejects gives `EBADF`), `O_CLOEXEC` (ignored by `open`) and `getpid` (1, the only process); no `mmap`, `munmap` or `getpagesize`. `LIBC_UNWIND_TABLES=1` adds `-funwind-tables` to the library (`common.mk`); the default build, its baselines and its budgets stay without it. With it every image links libgcc's EHABI personality routine and unwinder, which calls `abort`, so a freestanding image also needs `__rtos_abort` (`test/unit/freestanding` defines it only in that build). `port/none` has no linker script, so `rtos-none.h` states the `__exidx_start`/`__exidx_end` contract for a board's. The toolchain's `libstdc++exp.a` was built for newlib, and its headers need a complete `<wchar.h>` (out of scope, §1), so `<stacktrace>` cannot be compiled against this library's headers; P7.04 therefore tests capture through `_Unwind_Backtrace` itself (`test/unit/unwind`, Arm), which is the call `std::stacktrace::current()` makes, and the `std::stacktrace` test moves to P7.05 with a libstdc++ built against the library. | The capture path (unwind tables, the EHABI index, the unwinder) is the library's and the board's; the C++ layer above it depends on the toolchain build of Q39. |
| D135 | **The public headers stay C23-only; pi-pico moves to `-std=gnu23`** (answers Q40; the user's decision). `<assert.h>`, `<inttypes.h>`, `<stdatomic.h>`, `<stdbit.h>` and `<stddef.h>` keep their C23 syntax, with no C11 or C17 fallback and no version conditionals, so the conditional-compilation rule is unchanged. pi-pico, which builds with `-std=gnu11` today, switches to `-std=gnu23` when it builds against the library (P7.05). C consumers must compile as C23; C++ keeps D120 and D131. | The library is a C23 library (§1); one language mode keeps the headers free of version tests, and its first C consumer is moving anyway. |
| D136 | **Fixed-point double `sin`, `cos` and `sincos` in the speed flavour of the soft-double cores** (P8.03, phase 8; D133). A new libm layer, `dp-fixed`, holds only `speed/` files and is last in `LIBC_MATH_LAYERS` on armv6-m, armv6s-m, armv7-m, armv7e-m and armv8-m.main. Its kernel `__libc_trig_fixed` works as follows. **Reduction:** an integer reduction for |x| < 2²⁰ (2/π to 192 bits). **Polynomials:** Taylor polynomials for sin r / r and cos r in 64-bit fixed point. **Rounding:** a 64-bit result per function, rounded to nearest when it lies more than 18 units from a halfway point (measured largest error 7.08 units over 10⁸ arguments, analytic bound about 16.6; `tools/scripts/trig-fixed-check`). Otherwise the call falls back to the existing double-double path, as it does for |x| ≥ 2²⁰. **Results:** the fast path's results are correctly rounded, so in the rare case where the double-double path is not (it is within 0.53 ulp, D125), the speed flavour's `sin` and `cos` differ from the size flavour's, and are the better of the two; `sincos` stays bit-identical to its flavour's `sin` and `cos`. **Build:** the 32 × 32 → 64 multiply is chosen per core by an include directory picked in `speed.mk` (`umull/` inline, `muls/` calling nofp's `__libc_fixed_wide`), not by `#if`. The layer builds at `LIBC_SPEED_CFLAGS` (`-O2`), as `__libc_scan.o` does. The generic `libc/math/subdir.mk` now also drops a layer's `speed/` files in the speed flavour. The size flavour is unchanged. `tan` is unchanged. | The soft-double cores pay 6,400 (Armv7-M) to 17,700 (Armv6-M) instructions for a double-double `sin`; a fixed-point kernel with a rounding test is several times faster and correctly rounded where it answers. |
| D137 | **Phase 8 closed** (P8.04). Its gate holds: `sincos` and `sincosf` reduce once on every core (D133); double `sin`, `cos` and `sincos` run in fixed point in the speed flavour of the five soft-double cores (D136): `sin` 6.1× faster on Armv6-M and 8.0–8.1× on Armv7-M, Armv7E-M and Armv8-M.main; `cos` 4.8× and 5.8×; `sincos` 5.7× and 7.2–7.3× over P8.02; the D124 vectors pass at their bounds; `make check` passes in every configuration; both size budgets hold: the size flavour is unchanged, and the speed flavour holds after D138 re-locked `math-mix` on armv6-m, armv6s-m and armv7-m, the three cores with exact speed figures (D110). The plan's "about 10× on armv7e-m" is met by `sin` alone with no fallback in the bench. `cos` and `sincos` lose to one argument of the bench's 16 that falls back, and Armv6-M is limited by its 16-`MULS` 64-bit products. Follow-ups are Q41–Q44. | The phase's speedups are in, with their costs recorded; what remains is optional and measured. |
| D138 | **armv6-m's, armv6s-m's and armv7-m's speed figures re-locked for `math-mix`** (P8.03; amends D110, after D112 and D113). These three cores hold their speed-flavour images to exact figures, and the D136 kernel adds about 2 KB to `math-mix`, the only reference image that calls `sin` or `cos`, because the fixed-point code keeps the double-double cores for its fallback. The changes: armv6-m and armv6s-m 37,220 → 39,176 bytes (+1,956), armv7-m 33,520 → 35,196 (+1,676). Every other figure and every other image is unchanged. The speedup is 6.1× (`sin`) and 4.8× (`cos`) on Armv6-M and 8.0× and 5.8× on Armv7-M (`docs/perf/<arch>.md`). The local checks before the PR measured the release build by mistake (the `--build-type speed` flag was missing); CI's speed-budget step found the growth. | Re-locking under a decision entry is D110's procedure; the speed flavour exists to trade code for time, and 2 KB for a 5–8× faster `sin` and `cos` is that trade. |
| D139 | **Phase 9: Q41–Q43 in one phase** (the user's request after phase 8). The order is Q43 (`UMULL` for armv7-m's float fixed-point functions; a D110 re-lock if armv7-m's speed figures change), then Q41 (`sinf`, `cosf` and `sincosf` through the D136 reduction and kernel, rounded to float with a rounding test, on the cores whose speed flavour has nofp/speed), then Q42 (`tan` and `tanf` by a fixed-point reciprocal of the kernel's sine or cosine). Q44 is put to the user when the phase ends. | The three share the D136 kernel; Q43 is the smallest and changes the multiply the others also use on armv7-m. |
| D140 | **armv7-m's float fixed-point functions multiply by `UMULL`** (P9.01, answers Q43; re-locks two D110 figures). nofp/speed's `fixed.h` takes the 32 × 32 → 64 multiply from `fixed-wide.h`, an include directory `speed.mk` picks: `umull/` is an inline `UMULL` on armv7-m, and `muls/` declares the out-of-line 16-bit product helper (D86), which `subdir.mk` builds on Armv6-M only. On armv7-m's speed flavour 24 cases are faster: `powf` −57 %, `expf` −55 %, `exp2f` −50 %, `erff`/`erfcf` −46 %, `log10f` −44 %, `log2f` −37 %, `tanf` −20 %, `logf` −17 %, `sincosf` −16 %, `cosf` −13 %, `sinf` −10 %. No case is slower. The images shrink, so armv7-m's exact speed figures are re-locked downwards: `math-float` 11,580 → 11,412 and `math-mix` 35,196 → 35,024 bytes. Armv6-M is unchanged. | The core has the instruction; the helper existed only for Armv6-M. |
| D141 | **`sinf`, `cosf` and `sincosf` through the D136 kernel** (P9.02, answers Q41). On the cores whose speed flavour has nofp/speed (armv6-m, armv6s-m, armv7-m) the float functions first take the double kernel: `__libc_trig_fixed_from_float` builds the double from the bits and admits [2⁻²⁷, 2²⁰); `__libc_trig_fixed_roundf` rounds the 64-bit result to float, declining within the kernel's error bound of a halfway point at bit 40. The D87 path stays as the fallback (|x| ≥ 2²⁰, `cosf` below 2⁻²⁷, declined results); `sincosf` falls back per result and stays bit-identical to `sinf` and `cosf`. Every float in the range was checked on the host (`tools/scripts/trig-fixed-check --float`): 394,264,576 per function, none declined, none incorrectly rounded, with 1,077,017 results near a halfway point checked against MPFR (largest error 6.5 units). armv7e-m and armv8-m.main keep their single-precision FPU path, which is faster (about 570 instructions against the kernel's 800). | Phase 8 made the double kernel cheaper than the float one on these cores; a float is exact as a double, so the double result rounds to float with a test 2²² times wider than its error. |
| D142 | **`tan` and `tanf` through the D136 kernel; the soft cores' exact speed figures re-locked** (P9.03, answers Q42; re-locks D110 figures after D138 and D140). **Algorithm:** `__libc_tan_fixed` takes sin x and cos x from one kernel call and divides them: a 64-bit reciprocal of the cosine's significand by Newton's iteration from a 17-bit seed (one 32/16-bit division, `UDIV` on the Thumb-2 cores, `__aeabi_uidiv` on Armv6-M), then one high product. Its error is below 56 units of the 64-bit result: the measured largest is 12.5 over 10⁸ arguments, about 5.6 % of arguments fall back, and none is wrong. The analytic bound adds the sine's and cosine's errors, the reciprocal's and the product's. **Placement:** the speed `tan.c` (`dp-fixed`, five soft cores) falls back to `__libc_tan_dd`, tan.c's double-double path. nofp/speed's `tanf` takes the same quotient rounded to float, with every float checked on the host, none declined and none wrong. **Tests:** 16 near-halfway `tan` cases join `libm_dense` (a zero-bound mutant fails 10 of them; the other 6 lie exactly on the halfway point). **Speed** (instructions per call, speed flavour): `tan` 32,451 → 4,970 on armv6-m and about 11,200 → 1,000 on the Armv7-M, Armv7E-M and Armv8-M.main cores. **Size:** float programs now link the double kernel, and `tan` keeps its fallback, so armv6-m's, armv6s-m's and armv7-m's exact speed figures are re-locked: `math-float` 12,536 → 15,456 (Armv6-M) and 11,412 → 13,516 (armv7-m), `math-mix` 39,176 → 41,340 and 35,024 → 36,376. On armv6-m `tanf` gains only 1.5 % (its D87 divide was already short). | `tan` was the slowest trigonometric function on every soft core (11,000–32,000 instructions); the quotient reuses the kernel and its tests. |
| D143 | **The kernels' fallback stays as it is** (answers Q44; the user's decision after phase 9). The D136 kernel falls back to the double-double path on about 1.8 % of `sin` and `cos` arguments and 5.6 % of `tan`'s (D142), and always from 2²⁰ up. None of the four options is taken: a tighter proved bound, a 96-bit reduced argument, a jπ/32 table, or dropping the fallback, which would cost correct rounding. The speed flavour keeps correctly rounded fast-path results and the double-double fallback. | The remaining fallback cost is an average of a few hundred instructions per call, and each option adds code or analysis for it. |
| D144 | **What a hosted libstdc++ still needed (issue #113; amends D128, D131, D134)**. All four items are found building GCC 16.2's hosted libstdc++ against the library. **`stat`** (`std::filesystem`'s `ops-common.h` does `using ::stat`): `__rtos_open(path, READ)`, then D134's `fstat`, then close. A path the port cannot open (a directory; every path on `port/none`) gives the port's error. **`isatty` and `dup`** (`std::print`, C++23): the seam can neither tell a terminal nor duplicate a handle, so `isatty` returns 0 with `ENOTTY` (`EBADF` for a handle the port rejects, tested as `fcntl` does) and `dup` returns −1 with `ENOSYS` (`EBADF` likewise). libstdc++ then writes `std::print` output plainly, and reaches `dup` only for a terminal. This supersedes P7.01's "`isatty` is deferred", which held only before GCC 14's `<print>`. **`jmp_buf`'s struct is named `__jmp_buf_tag`**, as in glibc and newlib, so libstdc++'s `std` module can export `setjmp` and `longjmp`; the ABI is unchanged. **`unreachable()` is not defined in C++** (`#ifndef __cplusplus`, D120's category): libstdc++'s `std::unreachable()` in `<utility>` would otherwise be rewritten into a self-call. The other C23 headers with C++ counterparts (`<stdbit.h>`, `<stdckdint.h>`, `<stdatomic.h>`) are shadowed by libstdc++'s own; its `<stdatomic.h>` reaches ours only for Clang before C++23, and `<stdbit.h>` already guards its macros. | Each is the smallest truthful answer the seam allows, and each was confirmed against the hosted build (issue #113). |
| D145 | **`call_once` for C++: a separate exception-safe entry point** (issue #121; amends D43's `call_once`). libstdc++ on the toolchain uses `--enable-threads=c11`, so `std::call_once` is `call_once` with libstdc++'s `__once_proxy` as the function. When the callable throws, the exception must unwind through `call_once`, which has no unwind table (the library is built without `-fexceptions`), so the program terminated; and the flag stayed RUNNING, so every later call waited for ever. Building `call_once.c` with `-fexceptions` fixes both, but its cleanup landing pad references `_Unwind_Resume` and `__gcc_personality_v0` strongly, so every C program that calls `call_once` would link libgcc's unwinder: +4.4 KB of code on armv7e-m (`test/unit/none` 15,332 → 19,758 bytes), and on x86_64 a link against `libgcc_eh`. A weak reference cannot avoid it, because nothing in libsupc++ pulls in `__gcc_personality_v0`, so a C++ program would unwind into a null personality. Instead **`__libc_call_once_eh(once_flag *, void (*)(void))`**, declared in `<__once_flag.h>` beside `call_once` and built alone with `-fexceptions` (`libc/threads/threads.mk`), runs the function under a `cleanup` guard that, when an exception leaves it, puts the flag back to NOT_RUN and wakes the waiters; `call_once` stays table-free. Both share one state machine (`libc/internal/once.h`), in which a waiter that finds the flag back at NOT_RUN claims it, so the two can be mixed on one flag. The toolchain's `gthr-c11.h` `__gthread_once` calls `__libc_call_once_eh` (a patch in sgstreet/rtoskit-gcc); until then `std::call_once` still terminates when the callable throws. A `longjmp` out of the function runs no cleanup and leaves the flag RUNNING, as POSIX leaves `pthread_once`. | A C program that calls `call_once` pays nothing for C++'s exception rules, and C++ gets them with a one-line toolchain change. |
| D146 | **`getentropy` over a new seam entropy call** (issue #127; supersedes P7.03's "`getentropy` is not provided" in D132 and `docs/plan/phase-7.md`). The seam gains `int __rtos_entropy(void *buffer, size_t count)`: it fills all `count` bytes from the port's entropy source, blocking until the source is ready, and returns 0 or a negated errno (`-ENOSYS` without a source, `-EIO` when it fails); a port must never substitute predictable bytes. `getentropy(void *, size_t)` in `<unistd.h>` (POSIX.1-2024) fails with `EINVAL` above `GETENTROPY_MAX`, 256 in `<limits.h>` under D132's `__LIBC_POSIX_VISIBLE` (OpenBSD's and FreeBSD's error; glibc and musl use `EIO`), and otherwise passes the seam's error through. The `linux` port uses `getrandom(2)`, looping on `EINTR` and short reads; `port/none` has a weak default returning `-ENOSYS`, which a board with a hardware generator replaces. `docs/research/posix-subset.md` has the sources and edge cases. | libstdc++'s `std::random_device` gets a default source (`_GLIBCXX_HAVE_GETENTROPY`) on every port that has one, and fails honestly on one that has not. |
| D147 | **`<rtoskit-libc.h>`: the library's features for a toolchain's configure** (issue #125). libstdc++'s configure runs no link tests on a cross target, and rtoskit-gcc's `--with-rtoskit-libc` branch hard-codes 57 `AC_DEFINE`s about this library. Following libstdc++'s `--with-picolibc` branch, which compiles probes against `<picolibc.h>`, the library installs `include/rtoskit-libc.h`: `__RTOSKIT_LIBC__`, and `__LIBC_HAVE_<NAME>` defined as 1 for each of 64 features, `<NAME>` in autoconf's spelling so libstdc++'s `HAVE_<NAME>` (and `_GLIBCXX_USE_<NAME>` for `CLOCK_REALTIME`, `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, `NANOSLEEP`, `SCHED_YIELD`; `_GLIBCXX_HAVE___CXA_THREAD_ATEXIT`) maps by name: the patch's 57, `SYS_STAT_H`, `GETENTROPY`, `__CXA_THREAD_ATEXIT` and the four time names. One header serves every architecture and flavour archive (nothing listed varies, and D15 forbids per-multilib headers); whether a port has a clock or an entropy source is decided at link time and is not the header's to say. `test/unit/features` asserts every macro, links every function and calls the run-time ones on the Linux port, and its `.run` fails when the header names a feature the test does not check. The configure loop for rtoskit-gcc is in `docs/research/libstdcxx-features.md`. Preferred to the issue's data file because it is the form libstdc++ already uses for picolibc and needs no new configure option. | The statement of what the library provides lives with the library and is tested on every architecture; the toolchain asks instead of asserting. |
| D148 | **POSIX threads in the library, and `<threads.h>` over them** (issue #126, phase 10, `docs/research/pthreads.md`; supersedes D128's "`<pthread.h>` stays pi-pico's own", and D43's `<threads.h>` written directly on the seam). `libc.a` provides `<pthread.h>`: threads with attributes (stack size, a caller's stack, priority and policy, detach state), `pthread_once`, mutexes (normal, recursive, error-checking, default; timed and clock-selecting locks; priority inheritance where the port supports it), condition variables (with `pthread_condattr_setclock` and `pthread_cond_clockwait`), keys, read-write locks, barriers and spin locks. C11's `thrd_*`, `mtx_*`, `cnd_*`, `tss_*` and `call_once` become wrappers over them, which changes the layout of `mtx_t`, `cnd_t` and `once_flag` (the toolchain and pi-pico rebuild together; D15 holds). Out of scope: cancellation, `pthread_kill` and signals, `pthread_atfork`, process-shared objects (`ENOTSUP`) and robust mutexes. The seam changes (an attribute record at thread creation, a `void *` exit value so libc keeps no per-thread allocation, priorities, a clock for futex deadlines, a priority-inheritance lock pair) are decided in P10.02 and P10.05 (Q45, Q46). The toolchain's libstdc++ moves to `--enable-threads=posix` with `_GLIBCXX_GTHREAD_USE_WEAK 0`: outside glibc and Bionic, `gthr-posix.h` decides that threads are active from a weak reference to `pthread_cancel`, which a static link never resolves. | One thread implementation serves C, C++ and every port; pi-pico drops its own `rtos/pthread` and `rtos/threads`; libstdc++'s steady-clock waits use the POSIX clock-selecting calls instead of a C11 extension (issue #123). |
| D149 | **Priorities: a range per policy, and real round-robin** (answers Q45; P10.02). The seam reports a separate POSIX range for each policy it supports (`SCHED_FIFO`, `SCHED_RR`, `SCHED_OTHER`), with higher numbers more urgent, and `sched_get_priority_min/max` return them; an unsupported policy fails with `EINVAL`. `SCHED_RR` is real round-robin: the port time-slices threads of equal priority under it, rather than treating it as `SCHED_FIFO`. A thread's priority and policy are set at creation (`PTHREAD_EXPLICIT_SCHED`) and by `pthread_setschedparam`/`setschedprio`, in POSIX numbers that the port maps onto its scheduler. | The user's choice: each policy keeps its POSIX meaning, so code written for another POSIX system schedules the same way. |
| D150 | **`__rtos_futex_wait` takes a time base** (answers Q46; P10.02). The signature becomes `__rtos_futex_wait(address, expected, base, deadline)`, `base` being a seam time base (`TIME_UTC`, `TIME_MONOTONIC`), so a wait can end on `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` (`pthread_cond_clockwait`, `pthread_mutex_clocklock`, `pthread_condattr_setclock`). One call, not a second one beside it: the Linux port and `port/none` change with it, and pi-pico's port is not written yet. C11's timed waits pass `TIME_UTC`. | The user's choice: one wait call in the seam; the only ports to change are in this tree. |
| D151 | **No cancellation functions** (answers Q47). The library declares and defines none of `pthread_cancel`, `pthread_setcancelstate`, `pthread_setcanceltype`, `pthread_testcancel`, `PTHREAD_CANCELED` or the `PTHREAD_CANCEL_*` constants (D148's scope); pi-pico drops its declarations when it moves to the library's `<pthread.h>`, since nothing in it calls them. libstdc++ does not need `pthread_cancel` once built with `_GLIBCXX_GTHREAD_USE_WEAK 0` (D148). | The user's choice: no stubs that would suggest cancellation works. |
| D152 | **`<pthread.h>`'s thread functions** (P10.03; D148). The types live in `<__pthread_types.h>`, which `<pthread.h>` and `<sys/types.h>` include. `pthread_t` is the seam's handle, as `thrd_t` is. `pthread_create` passes a `struct __libc_pthread_start` (the routine and its argument, `malloc`ed) to a trampoline that ends in `pthread_exit`, and detaches a thread created detached; its errors are `EAGAIN` (memory, or a port without threads such as `port/none`), `EINVAL` and `EPERM`. `thrd_exit` and `pthread_exit` share `__libc_thread_exit` (the C++ `thread_local` and key destructors, then the seam's exit, or `exit(EXIT_SUCCESS)` for the last thread), and `__libc_thread_count` moves there. **`pthread_once` is table-free**, like `call_once` (D145), and `pthread_once_t` has `once_flag`'s layout: under libstdc++'s `posix` model `std::call_once` calls `__gthread_once`, which the toolchain must point at `__libc_call_once_eh` (P10.08) so that an exception from the callable resets the flag without making C callers of `pthread_once` link the unwinder. **`PTHREAD_KEYS_MAX` is 32**, below POSIX's minimum of 128 (`_POSIX_THREAD_KEYS_MAX`): each thread holds a value per key in its TLS block, and 128 would cost every thread 1 KiB on a 32-bit core; `<threads.h>`'s table already has 32 and pi-pico's 16. `PTHREAD_DESTRUCTOR_ITERATIONS` is 4 and `PTHREAD_STACK_MIN` 512 (the port may need more). `<unistd.h>` defines `_POSIX_THREADS`, `_POSIX_TIMEOUTS`, `_POSIX_THREAD_ATTR_STACKADDR`, `_POSIX_THREAD_ATTR_STACKSIZE` and `_POSIX_THREAD_PRIORITY_SCHEDULING` (202405L); `PTHREAD_SCOPE_PROCESS` is `ENOTSUP`. | No allocation beyond the start record, one exit path for both APIs, and the key table sized for small cores; the POSIX deviation is stated rather than hidden. |
| D153 | **`<pthread.h>`'s mutexes, condition variables and keys** (P10.04; D148, D150). Mutexes keep `<threads.h>`'s three-state futex word (`pthread_mutex_t` is `mtx_t`'s state, type, owner and count, plus a protocol) and the type decides the owner's relock (`RECURSIVE` counts, `ERRORCHECK` gives `EDEADLK`, `NORMAL` and `DEFAULT` deadlock as POSIX allows) and `trylock` (`EBUSY` unless recursive and held); every type's unlock by a stranger is `EPERM`, and `destroy` of a held mutex `EBUSY`. `timedlock` waits on `CLOCK_REALTIME` and `clocklock` on `CLOCK_REALTIME` or `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` (`EINVAL` otherwise), through D150's time base; a bad `tv_nsec` is `EINVAL` only when the call would wait. Condition variables keep the sequence counter, and store their clock (`pthread_condattr_setclock`: `CLOCK_REALTIME`, the default and `PTHREAD_COND_INITIALIZER`'s 0, or `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`); `timedwait` uses it, `clockwait` takes one, and waiting without the mutex is `EPERM`. Process-shared objects and `PTHREAD_PRIO_PROTECT` are `ENOTSUP`; `PTHREAD_PRIO_INHERIT` is `ENOTSUP` until P10.05. Keys share `<threads.h>`'s table and destructor passes (`TSS_DTOR_ITERATIONS` equals `PTHREAD_DESTRUCTOR_ITERATIONS`), so `tss_t` and `pthread_key_t` index the same `PTHREAD_KEYS_MAX` keys; `pthread_key_create` is `EAGAIN` when they are all in use, and `delete`/`setspecific` `EINVAL` for a key not in use. | One futex mutex and one condition design for both APIs, which P10.07 then merges. |
| D154 | **Priority-inheritance mutexes through a port lock** (P10.05; D148). The seam gains `__rtos_mutex_lock_pi(word, base, deadline)`, `__rtos_mutex_trylock_pi(word)` and `__rtos_mutex_unlock_pi(word)`: the word belongs to the port (libc only zeroes it, which is unlocked), and while a thread waits the owner runs at least at its priority. A `PTHREAD_PRIO_INHERIT` mutex hands its state word to them, and libc keeps the owner, count and type as for any mutex, so recursion, `EDEADLK` and `EPERM` behave the same; condition waits release and retake it through the same path. `pthread_mutexattr_setprotocol(PTHREAD_PRIO_INHERIT)` takes and releases a word of its own and gives `ENOTSUP` if the port's lock fails (`-ENOSYS` for a port without inheritance). The Linux port uses `FUTEX_LOCK_PI` (absolute `CLOCK_REALTIME`) or `FUTEX_LOCK_PI2` (`CLOCK_MONOTONIC`), with the caller's tid (cached per thread) in the word and the uncontended lock and unlock done without the kernel; qemu-user 8.2 implements both. `port/none` takes the word, and gives `-EDEADLK` for a word already held. `_POSIX_THREAD_PRIO_INHERIT` is not defined, since support depends on the port. The boost itself is the port's: the Linux test port runs unprivileged threads under `SCHED_OTHER`, so `test/unit/pthread` checks the locking, not the boost, which pi-pico's port must test on target. | Priority inheritance where the RTOS has it (pi-pico's scheduler does), with libc's mutex rules unchanged. |
| D155 | **Read-write locks, barriers and spin locks** (P10.06; D148, D150). A read-write lock is a state word (the number of readers, or a writer mark), the writer's handle and a sequence that every release freeing the lock advances and wakes; a waiter reads the sequence before the state, so no release is missed. **Readers go first**: a reader joins whenever no writer holds the lock, so a thread holding a read lock can always take another, as POSIX allows, but a steady stream of readers can starve a writer. The writer's own relock and read lock are `EDEADLK`; a read lock by a thread that reads is not detected when it then asks to write (it deadlocks, which POSIX allows); readers are not tracked by thread, so any reader's unlock releases one read lock; an unlock with no holder, or by a thread that is not the writer, is `EPERM`; `EAGAIN` at the most readers. `timed*lock` waits on `CLOCK_REALTIME`, `clock*lock` on a chosen clock. A barrier counts arrivals; the last of `count` resets them, advances the generation the others sleep on and returns `PTHREAD_BARRIER_SERIAL_THREAD`. A spin lock is an exchanged word that yields to the scheduler between tries, so that on one core a lower-priority holder can run and release it. Process-shared objects are `ENOTSUP` throughout. | Small, futex-based designs with POSIX's errors, and no priority inversion through a spin lock on a single core. |
| D156 | **`<threads.h>` is a layer over `<pthread.h>`** (P10.07; supersedes D43's `<threads.h>` written directly on the seam). `thrd_t` is `pthread_t` (the seam's handle, unchanged); `mtx_t` holds a `pthread_mutex_t` (`mtx_recursive` a recursive one, otherwise normal) and its C11 type, which `mtx_timedlock` checks for `mtx_timed`; `cnd_t` holds a `pthread_cond_t` on `CLOCK_REALTIME`; `tss_t` and `pthread_key_t` name the same keys, and `tss_*` call `pthread_key_*`; `call_once` and `pthread_once` already share one state machine (D152). `thrd_create` and `pthread_create` share `__libc_pthread_create`: the start record names a POSIX or a C11 routine, so each thread still costs one `malloc`, and both kinds end in `__libc_thread_exit`. The C11 return codes map from the POSIX errors (`EBUSY` to `thrd_busy`, `ETIMEDOUT` to `thrd_timedout`, a port's `-ENOMEM` to `thrd_nomem`, anything else to `thrd_error`). **The layout of `mtx_t` and `cnd_t` changes** (each grows by a word or two), so code compiled against the old `<threads.h>`, the toolchain's libstdc++ and pi-pico included, must be rebuilt with the library (D148). `test/unit/threads` and `test/unit/none` pass unchanged. The build's dependency files do not track the library's own public headers, so after a change to one, test objects must be rebuilt (`make clean`) as D117 asks for removed sources. | One implementation under both APIs, as musl does, with C11's behaviour kept. |
| D157 | **Re-lock of the exact speed figures after the thread seam's changes** (phase 10; supersedes D138's speed figures for armv6-m, armv6s-m and armv7-m and the size-flavour figures of armv7-m and armv8-m.main, under D110). The reference programs link the Linux test port, whose `__rtos_futex_wait` now rejects a time base it does not have (D150) and whose `__rtos_lock_acquire` passes one: each of the seven programs that take the port's lock grows by 12 bytes on armv6-m and armv6s-m and 16 on armv7-m, in both flavours, with the library's share of every image unchanged. The speed figures of those three cores, which D110 holds exactly, are locked again from this build (`size-report --build-type speed budget --flavour speed --lock`); The size-flavour figures of armv7-m and armv8-m.main, also held exactly, grow by 4 bytes in the same programs and are locked again from this build too (`size-report budget --arch A --lock`, one core at a time, merging only that core's images); armv7e-m and armv7e-m-dp stay within their budgets. The size baselines record the same images (`docs/size/<arch>.md`, phase 10). | The growth is the test port's contract, not the library's code; a real port pays for its own futex. |
| D158 | **Releases** (`docs/release.md`): a release is a signed, annotated tag `vX.Y.Z` on `main` (semantic versioning; while the major number is 0 a minor release may break the ABI or the seam), pushed by the maintainer. `.github/workflows/release.yml` then runs the pull request gate (D119) on the tagged commit for every architecture, packages each architecture's `release` and `speed` builds (`make library`: headers, `libc*.a`, `libm.a`, the port archives) with `tools/scripts/release-package` into `rtoskit-libc-X.Y.Z-<arch>.tar.xz`, writes `SHA256SUMS` and a build-provenance attestation per archive, and creates a draft release (a pre-release for `0.x` and `-rc` versions). The notes are the hand-written `docs/release/vX.Y.Z.md`, which the workflow requires, followed by GitHub's generated list of the pull requests since the previous published release, grouped by label (`.github/release.yml`); the first release, with no previous one, has the hand-written notes alone. The maintainer signs off twice: the signed tag (the code) and publishing the draft (the release); the workflow never publishes. The first release is v0.1.0, at the end of phase 10's P10.07. | Prebuilt archives save users the toolchain and the build; a draft keeps a human between CI and a public release, and a signed tag ties the release to its maintainer. |
| D159 | **The first release is v0.5.0** (supersedes D158's v0.1.0): the maintainer's choice of number; the notes are `docs/release/v0.5.0.md`. | The library is complete for its scope but its ABI is not yet frozen; 0.5 says so better than 0.1. |
| D160 | **`LIBC_EXTRA_CFLAGS`, the parent's compiler flags** (issue #136): `tools/makefiles/common.mk` appends `${LIBC_EXTRA_CFLAGS}` (exported, empty by default) to `LIBC_CFLAGS`, after the toolchain file's own flags and `LIBC_UNWIND_TABLES`'s `-funwind-tables` (D134), so a parent build (rtoskit-gcc: `-funwind-tables -mpoke-function-name` for every multilib) adds flags without restating the library's. It reaches every module that takes `LIBC_CFLAGS`: the C sources of `libc.a`, its flavour archives and the port archives; not the assembler sources (`ASFLAGS`), the link or the test programs. `CFLAGS`, `LIBC_CFLAGS` or `CROSS_FLAGS` on the command line still replace the library's flags and stay unsupported for this, as does the `CC="gcc <flags>"` workaround. Like `LIBC_MALLOC` it needs `make clean` after a change; the size baselines and budgets are without it. | One documented variable for the parent's flags, kept apart from the flags the library depends on. |
| D161 | **`<stdlib.h>`'s `once_flag` is C only** (issue #138; extends D120's list of `#ifndef __cplusplus` uses). C23 7.24p2 puts `once_flag`, `ONCE_FLAG_INIT` and `call_once` in `<stdlib.h>` as well as `<threads.h>`; libstdc++'s `<cstdlib>` includes `<stdlib.h>`, so in C++ the global `::once_flag` and `::call_once` met `std::once_flag` and `std::call_once` from `<mutex>`, and `using namespace std;` made `once_flag` ambiguous. `<stdlib.h>` now includes `<__once_flag.h>` only in C. `<threads.h>`, which C++ does not have and a program includes only for C's names, still declares them; `<pthread.h>`, which `gthr-posix.h` includes, never declared them. An audit of every public header found no other global name that namespace `std` declares differently: `unreachable()` (D144) and the C-only bodies D120 lists were already guarded, and every header that declares functions has `__LIBC_BEGIN_DECLS`. `test/unit/cxx/std_names.cpp` includes every public header but `<threads.h>`, declares stand-ins for `std::once_flag`, `std::call_once` and `std::unreachable`, and uses them unqualified after `using namespace std;`. | C++ has its own spelling of these; the C names only collide with it. |
| D162 | **Phase 11: the `fast` build type** (the maintainer's request; P11.01, `docs/research/fast-math.md`, `docs/plan/phase-11.md`). A fourth build type, `fast` (`tools/makefiles/fast.mk`, in `BUILDS`), is the speed flavour (D73) with a faster libm. It builds into `build/<arch>/fast/` and installs into `images/<arch>/fast/`. A new exported `LIBC_FLAVOURS` lists the flavour directories in increasing priority (`speed`: `speed`; `fast`: `speed fast`), and every makefile that selects `speed/` files or archives filters it instead of `LIBC_FLAVOUR`, so `fast` falls back to `speed`'s kernels (`LIBC_STRING_KERNELS_fast := ${LIBC_STRING_KERNELS_speed}`) and `speed/` files, then `libc/math/fast/` and `libc/math/<layer>/fast/` files replace same-named ones (D77's rule; precedence `<layer>/fast/` > `fast/` > `<layer>/speed/` > `<layer>/` > generic). `fast` relaxes only accuracy and rounding. **`errno` and the floating-point exceptions are kept as in the other build types, on every core, the soft-`<fenv.h>` cores included** (the maintainer's decision, resolving Q48): `math_errhandling` stays `MATH_ERREXCEPT`, so libm leaves `errno` alone as it does in every build type (D45), and invalid, divide-by-zero, overflow and deserved underflow are raised by the same helpers as in speed. **Rounding is to nearest only:** the functions compute as if to nearest and never read or set the mode; in a directed mode (x86_64, armv7e-m-dp) no error bound is promised. Accuracy, pending Q49: below 1 ulp to nearest for exp, log, pow, trig and inverse trig, below 1.5 ulp for `tan`, the hyperbolic functions and `erf`, the rest as in speed. Kept in every mode: the headers (`__STDC_IEC_559__` as D44), F.10's special values, signed zeros, overflow and underflow results, gradual underflow, bounded ranges, symmetry, exact argument reduction and the exact functions. The tests keep every debug, release and speed check unchanged and select the fast contract's expectations by flavour files (`test/unit/libm_vectors/fast/bounds.c`), never by `#if` (Q51). `make bench` gates `fast` on `docs/perf/baseline-<arch>-fast.json`, and `size-report` budgets it as the speed flavour (D73). | The measured gap (README, `docs/perf/phase-3.md`, `phase-4.md`: picolibc 0.39 and AOR 0.16–0.27 of our instructions for double `exp`, `log`, `pow` on armv7e-m) is almost all the double-double and float-float evaluation behind correct rounding; on the five cores without a double FPU, exceptions and directed modes cost nothing today, because those cores have neither (D44). C23 7.12 and F.10 make accuracy, the rounding direction and inexact implementation-defined or unspecified, so a less accurate libm needs no header change. A build type rather than a flavour archive was asked for, and is what a toolchain or parent build selects most simply (Q50). |
| D163 | **Phase 11's open questions answered** (the maintainer's answers to Q49–Q51 and the Q48 reading; amends D162). (1) Q49: the D162 accuracy bounds stand (below 1 ulp to nearest for exp, log, pow, trig and inverse trig, including `powf`; below 1.5 ulp for `tan`, the hyperbolic functions and `erf`), and the `fast/` sources drop `-frounding-math` while keeping `-fsignaling-nans` and the default `-ftrapping-math`. (2) Q50: `fast` stays a build type, not a flavour archive; the release archives (D158) gain the `fast` tree only once P11.06 meets the phase's exit criterion, and leave it out until then. (3) Q51: each test that checks the strict contract gets a `fast/` expectation file with the fast contract, keeping every check that is not about the last bit; the strict files are unchanged and still run in debug, release and speed. (4) Q48's reading is confirmed: no build type sets `errno` from `<math.h>` (`MATH_ERREXCEPT`, D45), `fast` included. | `-frounding-math` only protects directed results, which `fast` no longer promises; trapping math stays because 7.12.1p1 forbids spurious invalid, divide-by-zero and overflow. A build type is what was asked for and what a parent build selects most simply; shipping it before it is faster than speed would only grow the archives. The split keeps every passing test unchanged in the build types whose contract it checks. |
| D164 | **The `fast` build type, built** (P11.02a, `docs/research/fast-math.md` §7 and §9; realises D162, D163; amends D117). `tools/makefiles/fast.mk` is `speed.mk` with `-DBUILD_TYPE_FAST` and `export LIBC_FLAVOUR := fast`, `LIBC_FLAVOURS := speed fast` (`speed.mk` exports `LIBC_FLAVOURS := speed`; debug and release leave it empty); `BUILDS` and `make bench` gain `fast`; each arch file sets `LIBC_STRING_KERNELS_fast := ${LIBC_STRING_KERNELS_speed}`. Every `speed/` selection (`libc/libc.mk`, `libc/math/subdir.mk`, `libc/math/sp-nearest/subdir.mk`, `libc/stdio/subdir.mk`, `libc/stdlib/subdir.mk`) filters `LIBC_FLAVOURS`; `libc/libc.mk` archives `libc/math[/<layer>]/<flavour>/` for each listed flavour whose module exists. `tools/makefiles/common.mk` gives `LIBC_MATH_FAST_STEMS`, the stems of `libc/math/fast/` and `libc/math/<layer>/fast/` when `LIBC_FLAVOURS` has `fast` (else empty), and `libc_math_fast_filter`, which the generic `libc/math`, `sp`, `sp-nearest`, `nofp/speed` and `dp-fixed/speed` (a new `subdir.mk`) apply to their `SRC`; `libc/math/fast/` is a module of every build type (`math.mk`'s `SUBDIRS`), compiled without `-frounding-math` (D163), whose `subdir.mk` drops the stems of the layers' `fast/`; a `<layer>/fast/` module drops those of later layers' `fast/`, and a layer without a `subdir.mk` gets one when a `fast/` file first shadows one of its files. **No two `libc.a` members may share a name**: `ar` keeps both and the link takes the first, so `libc.unique` (a prerequisite of `libc.a` and the flavour archives) stops the build on a duplicate; D117's stale shadowed object, which was linked silently, now fails the build with the name until it is deleted or the architecture cleaned. Tools: `size-report same --as <type>` compares two build types' `libc.a` member by member, every section but the debug ones (with `-g3`'s `wm4.*` macro-group names masked); `size-report` budgets `fast` as speed (`margins_percent`, `floor_bytes`, `program_margins_percent` and armv6-m's, armv6s-m's and armv7-m's own figures copied from speed in `docs/size/budget.json`); `bench-runner` keeps `docs/perf/baseline-<arch>-fast.json` and, with `--build-type fast`, adds a fast column to `compare` and `aor` and takes it as the ratios' reference (`aor-<arch>-fast.json`). Tests: `test/unit/libm_vectors` asks `libm_exact_result_required` and `libm_within_bound` of `bounds.c` (debug, release, speed: today's expressions, the same checks) or `fast/bounds.c` (fast: below 1 or 1.5 ulp to nearest for the relaxed families, a representable exact result within the bound but a zero bit for bit; in the directed modes the mode's overflow result and the correctly rounded sign; the rest as speed), chosen by its `subdir.mk`; special values, exact functions and exceptions stay in the shared source. `flavours.run` checks that each build type installs exactly `include/`, and `flavours.c` that `math_errhandling` is `MATH_ERREXCEPT`. CI and the release workflow add the fast budget; `release-package` and `docs/release.md` leave the `fast` tree out until P11.06 (D163). P11.02 is split: P11.02b audits the eight strict-contract tests (D163 (3)). Measured: with no `fast/` file the fast `libc.a` equals the speed one on all seven architectures (`size-report same`), the debug, release and speed archives are byte-identical to the ones built before the change, and `make check` passes in all 28 configurations; the 28 configurations take 23.7 minutes with `make -j4 check` on a 4-core cloud session (21 configurations before, about 18 by the same per-configuration time), still under Q33's hour, so `ARCHS` and `BUILDS` stay whole; `size-report diff` shows no growth and the size, speed and fast budgets hold on every Arm architecture; the release and speed benchmark counts are those of the commit before (the same `libc.a`): `make bench` fails as it did before, on 5 to 7 `threads` cases per architecture (`mtx_*` +10 to +40 %), the cost of P10.07's `<threads.h>` over `<pthread.h>` against baselines recorded at P8.02 (Q52); the fast baselines are the measured fast counts, equal case for case to the speed build's. On armv7e-m, `bench-runner --build-type fast compare` gives the phase's starting point: maths throughput (geometric mean of 128 cases) picolibc 0.44 and newlib-nano 0.47 of the fast build's instructions. | One list of flavour directories gives `fast` the speed flavour's code with no copy and leaves the other build types' makefiles reading the same files; the duplicate check is what makes shadowing by a third level of directories safe to rely on. |
| D165 | **The strict-contract tests' fast expectations: the audit and the uniform tests** (P11.02b, `docs/research/fast-math.md` §10; realises D163 (3)). A test whose checks depend on the build type's contract keeps them in its source and asks an expectation file, `expect.c` (debug, release, speed: exactly the comparisons it made before) or `fast/expect.c` (fast), which the module's `subdir.mk` selects on `LIBC_FLAVOURS`; the object compiles into `fast/` of the build directory by an order-only rule in the module's makefile. `test/unit/include/fast_contract.h` (header only, not a module) holds the relaxed families with their bounds (§4.2) and a float result's error in ulps, for every `fast/` file, `libm_vectors/fast/bounds.c` included. Of the eight tests D163 named, `fpu_math` and `float_exact` check exact functions only and need no `fast/` file; `float_hard`, `float_accuracy` and `libm_dense` have one now: in fast a relaxed function is held to its bound to nearest (`float_hard`'s hard cases: the two floats around the exact value, and the far neighbour under a 1.5 ulp bound; `float_accuracy`: every result and the worst below the bound, no count of results not correctly rounded, a zero exactly; `libm_dense`: within the bound of the double function, and a double near-halfway case the correctly rounded value or a neighbour), in a directed mode only to a finite result of the right sign, with inexact still required where the result is inexact; `erfcf`, `lgammaf`, `tgammaf` stay strict. `libm_exp`, `libm_trig` and `libm_special`, whose checks are inline and of every kind, are P11.02c. The fast helpers compare signs as `!signbit(a) == !signbit(b)`: `signbit` returns some nonzero value, not the same one for float and double, and the direct comparison failed every negative result. Measured: `make check` passes in all 28 configurations; with no `fast/` libm file the fast expectations pass as the strict ones. | The same split as the libm vectors' bounds (D164): the strict comparisons stay byte-for-byte what they were, and the fast contract lives in one file per test plus one shared table, so each later `fast/` libm file is checked at the contract without touching the strict tests. |
| D166 | **The release and speed bench baselines re-locked for phase 10's `threads` cost** (the maintainer's answer to Q52; issue #144). Since P10.07 put `<threads.h>` over `<pthread.h>` (D156), the uncontended `mtx_*` path goes through `pthread_mutex_lock` and `__libc_pthread_mutex_lock`, asks the seam for `__rtos_thread_self()`, tests the type and protocol and records the owner and count: `make bench` failed on every architecture in 7 `threads` cases against baselines recorded at P8.02. `docs/perf/baseline-<arch>.json` and `baseline-<arch>-speed.json` are re-locked with `BENCH_UPDATE=1` on all seven architectures; only those 7 cases change, and nothing else in any baseline (armv7e-m, armv7-m, armv7e-m-dp: `mtx/plain` and `mtx/timed` 84 → 107, `mtx/recursive` 87 → 107, `mtx/trylock` 78 → 103, `mtx/nested` 147 → 184, `call_once/done` 19 → 21, `tss_get` 34 → 35; armv8-m.main 82 → 105 for `mtx/plain`; armv6-m and armv6s-m 205 → 228; x86_64 92 → 116). The cost is recorded as phase 10's in `docs/perf/<arch>.md`. `make bench` passes again for release, speed and fast on every architecture. Winning the cost back, a direct uncontended path for plain C11 and normal POSIX mutexes with no owner bookkeeping, is issue #144. | A gate that fails on a known, accepted cost hides the next regression; the cost stays visible in the perf notes and in #144. |
| D167 | **The inline-check tests' fast expectations** (P11.02c, `docs/research/fast-math.md` §11; completes D163 (3) with D165). `libm_exp`, `libm_trig` and `libm_special` include `libm_expect.h`, found by include path: `test/unit/include/strict/` in debug, release and speed (exactly the comparisons the tests made: `==`, their bound table, `fabs(...) <=`, every exception bit) or `test/unit/include/fast/` in fast (the module's makefile filters `LIBC_FLAVOURS`); `static inline` functions, since the three tests share one bound table: `libm_vector_within`, `libm_exact`/`libm_exactf`, `libm_directed`, `libm_near` and `libm_exact_flags` with the tests' `EXPECT_EXACT`. 104 checks go through them (58, 43 and 3): the reference vectors, the exact results F.10 does not fix (`exp2(k)`, `exp10(k)`, `log2(2^k)`, `log10(10^k)`, `cbrt(27)`, `rootn`, `pow`'s, `compoundn`'s and the inverse and hyperbolic functions' exact or saturated values), the directed results and the `compoundn` tolerances. In fast, for a relaxed function, a vector is held below its bound to nearest and to the right sign directed; an exact result to the value or a neighbour, a zero exactly; a directed result to the right sign and the mode's overflow result exactly; a tolerance one ulp wider; and a check of exact results no longer compares inexact. F.10's special values, signed zeros, NaNs, overflow, results that round to zero, ranges, every exception and every function outside the relaxed families stay in the shared source. Measured: `make check` passes in all 28 configurations, the three tests in every build type on x86_64 and armv7e-m-dp (the cores with directed modes). P11.02 is done. | One header pair keeps the three tests' last-bit expectations in one place per contract, and the strict one is the old comparisons verbatim, so the build types that keep the strict contract check exactly what they did. |
| D168 | **Fast `expf`, `exp2f`, `exp10f`; precedence by layer; fast layers per core** (P11.03a, `docs/research/libm-fast-float-exp-log.md`; supersedes D162's precedence, amends D164). **Precedence:** D162's order (`<layer>/fast/` > `fast/` > `<layer>/speed/` > `<layer>/` > generic) let a generic or early-layer `fast/` file displace a later layer's faster code, the fixed-point `nofp/speed` files of the cores without an FPU. The order is now by layer first: generic < `fast/` < each layer of `LIBC_MATH_LAYERS` in turn, and within a layer its files < `speed/` < `fast/` (`common.mk`: `libc_math_fast_filter` takes the module's layer, `libc_math_layer_stems` the files a fast file gives way to; `libc/math/fast/` drops what any layer provides, a `<layer>/fast/` what a later layer provides). **Fast layers per core:** a layer's `fast/` directory applies only where the arch file lists the layer in `LIBC_MATH_FAST_LAYERS` (as `LIBC_STRING_KERNELS_*` choose kernels): armv7e-m and armv8-m.main list `sp-nearest`; the float-only files cost the cores without an FPU six software `fmaf` calls (`exp10f` 11,103 → 15,885 instructions on armv6-m, +43 %), so those cores keep speed's files until P11.03e. **The functions:** `libc/math/fast/{expf,exp2f,exp10f}.c` (x86_64, armv7e-m-dp) round once a double 2^t from a 32-entry table of 2^(j/32) and a degree 3 Taylor polynomial (`__libc_fast_exp2`, relative error 2^-30.7); `libc/math/sp-nearest/fast/` (armv7e-m, armv8-m.main) computes in float: an exact Cody–Waite high part by a fused multiply-add, the low parts of the constants, and a degree 5 polynomial for (e^u − 1 − u)/u² (`__libc_fast_expf_kernel`, 2^-30.3), refusing double helpers as the layer does. Both are in `libc/internal/math-fast.h`. **Exceptions:** without `-frounding-math` (D163) GCC folds the `2^-100 · 2^-100` underflow idiom to a constant that raises nothing (54 `libm_vectors` exception failures on x86_64 and armv7e-m-dp in the first build); the double files return `(float)__libc_math_underflow(1.0)`, the float files call `__libc_math_raise_underflow()` (D45's helpers). `test/unit/libm_thresholds` checks the special values, the overflow and underflow thresholds and their exceptions in every build type; `test/bench/math` gains `exp10`/`exp10f`. Measured: every float argument on the host, the double path at most 0.5096 ulp and the float path at most 0.8527 ulp (`expf`), 0.8443 (`exp2f`), 0.8472 (`exp10f`); instructions per call `expf` 160 → 58 and `exp2f` 141 → 59 on armv7e-m and armv8-m.main, 172 → 52 and 195 → 50 on armv7e-m-dp, 237 → 48 and 302 → 47 on x86_64; `exp10f` 160 → 66, 188 → 52 and 284 → 48. | A fast file must never be slower than what it replaces on a core; ordering by layer keeps every core's most specific code first, and the per-core list keeps code that needs an FPU off the cores that emulate it. |
| D169 | **Fast `logf`, `log2f`, `log10f`** (P11.03b, `docs/research/libm-fast-float-exp-log.md` §7–9). On x86_64 and armv7e-m-dp (`libc/math/fast/`): x = 2^e m, m in [√½, √2), log m = 2 atanh((m − 1)/(m + 1)) as a Taylor series to s^13 in double (`__libc_fast_log_double`, relative error 2^-39.5, no table), the base's constants in double, one rounding to float. On armv7e-m and armv8-m.main (`libc/math/sp-nearest/fast/`, float only): x = 2^k z against 32 entries (`__libc_fast_logf_reduce`), r = z/c_i − 1 by a fused multiply-add, the result HI + (r + LO) with HI = k ln2_hi + log c_i's high part exact (both multiples of 2^-16), log1p(r) − r by a degree 3 polynomial; four tables of 896 bytes in all, one object each. Within 2^-5 of 1 the reduction takes r = x − 1 exactly with c = 1: next to the entry holding 1, log c_i and r cancelled and the first build measured 1.93–2.72 ulp at x ≈ 0.996. The double files return +0 for x = 1: rounding downward m − 1 is −0 and the result was −0, against F.10.3.7 (`libm_vectors` found it; `libm_thresholds` now checks log(1) downward and the logarithms' special values, exceptions, range ends and exact powers of two in every build type). Measured on every positive float: the double path at most 0.5000 ulp, the float path 0.7507 (`logf`), 0.7943 (`log2f`), 0.8341 (`log10f`). The cores without an FPU keep the speed files (D168). | A table halves the float path's polynomial and keeps its one large rounding last; in double no table is needed. |
| D170 | **Fast `powf`** (P11.03c, `docs/research/libm-fast-float-exp-log.md` §10–11). The C23 F.10.4.5 special cases in one helper for every fast `powf` (`__libc_fast_powf_special`: the parity by truncf, every |y| from 2^24 up even, ±∞ for 0 to a negative power by a run-time division). On x86_64 and armv7e-m-dp (`libc/math/fast/`): t = y (e + log m/ln2) in double with `__libc_fast_log_double` and `__libc_fast_exp2`, one rounding to float; beyond ±1000 the overflow and underflow helpers. On armv7e-m and armv8-m.main (`libc/math/sp-nearest/fast/`, float only): log2|x| as hi + lo to 2^-39 absolute (`__libc_fast_log2f_extended`: section 8's tables without the near-1 short cut, r = z/c_i − 1 as r + p_lo exactly, p_lo/(1 + r) as p_lo (1 − r + r²), the products by 1/ln2 with their errors, log2 c_i as a full float and its remainder, exact two-sums), t = y·hi with its error by a fused multiply-add, k = round(t), e^((f + t_lo) ln2) 2^k by `__libc_fast_expf_kernel`; t ≥ 128.5 and t ≤ −151 by the raise helpers. The first build kept p_lo (1 − r): 1.34 ulp at x ≈ 0.996, |y| ≈ 2^14. Measured over 1.6 billion samples (four groups, x near 1 with |y| to 2^18 among them): the double path at most 0.5096 ulp, the float path 0.8749. The strict `pow` returned +1 for (−1)^odd beyond 2^20 and rounded ±1 to the power of a large y downward to 1 − 2^-53 (its tiny-z path kept neither the sign nor exactness): its exact power-of-two path now bounds the power y·ilogb(x) by 2^20 instead of |y|, so ±1 to any integer power is exact there, at no cost in size (`regression_pow_minus_one`; `libm_thresholds` checks every F.10.4.5 case, powf's overflow and underflow thresholds and exceptions in every build type). The cores without an FPU keep the speed files (D168). | The product's error grows with t, so float arithmetic carries log2|x| as a pair; in double one product is enough. |
| D171 | **Fast `expm1f`, `exp2m1f`, `exp10m1f`, `log1pf`, `log2p1f`, `log10p1f`, `cbrtf`** (P11.03d, `docs/research/libm-fast-float-exp-log.md` §12–14). Each keeps its accuracy relative to the result near 0. On x86_64 and armv7e-m-dp (`libc/math/fast/`, double, one rounding): the m1 forms by the Taylor series of e^u − 1 to u⁶ within |u| ≤ 1/8 (`__libc_fast_expm1_series`) and `__libc_fast_exp2` − 1 beyond (at most 0.5473 ulp); the p1 forms from x itself within 1/4 of 0 (`__libc_fast_log1p_double`) and 1 + x, exact there, beyond (0.5000 ulp; forming 1 + x everywhere measured up to 2.24 ulp near 2^-30); `cbrtf` from a degree 3 start and one Halley step (0.5000 ulp; plain products, since `__builtin_fma` is a software call on x86_64's baseline: 1,057 instructions in the first build). Below 2^-90 `exp10m1f`, `log2p1f` and `log10p1f` scale x by 2^64 (0.98–0.9999 ulp near FLT_MIN before). Every float argument measured. `__libc_fast_expf_q` is now 1/2 + u `__libc_fast_expf_q3`(u): `expf`, `exp2f`, `exp10f` and `powf` compute the same values (one fused multiply-add's operands swap). `libm_thresholds` checks the seven functions' special values, thresholds and near-0 results in every build type; `test/bench/math` gains `exp2m1`, `exp10m1`, `log2p1`, `log10p1`. The cores without an FPU keep the speed files (D168). | The m1 and p1 forms are where an error relative to 1 is not one relative to the result: the leading terms go exact, and the double path never forms 1 + x near 0. |

| D186 | **The fast double functions: the plan** (P11.05a, `docs/research/libm-fast-double.md`). Today the fast build's doubles are the speed files, which finish in double-double and test the rounding. On the double-FPU cores they cost 2.2–3.5 times AOR's `exp`, `exp2`, `log`, `log2` and `pow`, and 6 times its `erf` (`exp` 169 instructions on armv7e-m-dp against 56). On the soft-double cores they cost 2.6–5.6 times AOR's (`exp` 3813 on armv7e-m against 1090, 10,317 on armv6-m against 3954). P11.06 gates on being at or below AOR's for those. **The double-FPU cores** (x86_64, armv7e-m-dp) get double-only evaluations in AOR's structure (a 2^(j/N) or 1/c, log c table and a short polynomial, the last operation rounding), written clean-room in `libc/math/fast/`; pow keeps a tail on the logarithm. **The soft-double cores** (armv6-m, armv6s-m, armv7-m, armv7e-m, armv8-m.main) get 64-bit fixed-point kernels with one final rounding and no test or fallback, in a new `libc/math/dp-fixed/fast/` listed in their `LIBC_MATH_FAST_LAYERS`: a 64 × 64 → 128 product's high word costs about four 32 × 32 → 64 products (about 12 instructions on armv7-m, 80 on armv6-m), against one soft `dmul` per double operation (54 and 264), so the same polynomial costs 3–5 times less; Qfplib-M0-full's fixed-point `exp` (811 Cortex-M0 cycles against GCC's 6655) shows the scale. The target is 0.55 ulp, verified on at least 10^9 sampled arguments per function plus every piece and edge against long double, and by the libm vectors at the fast bounds. P11.05 is split into a–o: per family (exp, log, pow and cbrt, trigonometric, inverse, hyperbolic, erf), the FPU cores and the soft-double cores apart, AOR's functions first. | The two core classes share only the contract: the FPU cores are limited by the number of double operations and the soft-double cores by the cost of each one, and fixed point removes both the soft operations and the intermediate roundings. |
| D187 | **Fast `exp`, `exp2`, `exp10` and `expm1` in double, and the fast size figures during P11.05** (P11.05b, `docs/research/libm-fast-double.md` §6). `libc/math/fast/`, for every core with no layer of its own (the soft-double cores until P11.05c): 2^(k/64) from a 64-entry table of the correctly rounded value and its relative tail side by side (`__libc_fast_exp_pairs`, 1 KiB); the reductions exact (ln 2/64 and log10(2)/64 in two parts, the first short enough that k times it is exact); e^u − 1 and 2^r − 1 by degree-3 fits (2^-57.7) run by Horner's rule with the tail in the last step, and one rounding, scale + scale·tmp. The common range (2^-54 to 512, 256 for exp10) is guarded by one integer test on the exponent; the slow path, out of line, is the strict file's (`__libc_exp_core`, which pow shares; exp2's integers by `scalbn`), so a program that calls both carries no second copy of the ends. expm1 sums 2^(k/64) − 1, 2^(k/64)·hi as an exact product (fma or Dekker) and the small terms as pairs, with its own degree-5 fit, because those terms cancel near ln 2/128. Measured on 10^9 samples per function and every binade: at most 0.553 ulp. Instructions per call on armv7e-m-dp: `exp` 169 → 53 (AOR 56), `exp2` 183 → 51 (AOR 52), `exp10` 185 → 55, `expm1` 172 → 106; on x86_64 230 → 51, 287 → 47, 277 → 52, 242 → 134; on the soft-double cores `exp` 3813 → 1170 on armv7e-m (AOR 1090) and 10,317 → 3648 on armv6-m (AOR 3954). **Size, the maintainer's decision:** `math-mix`, which calls exp and pow, grows while pow still pulls in the strict core (+1212 bytes on armv6-m and armv6s-m and +1176 on armv7-m against their locked fast figures, +25.8 % over speed on armv7e-m-dp, whose budget was +20 %). Through P11.05 the fast figures may grow: each step re-locks the fast figures of the cores it grows (`size-report --flavour fast --lock`) and records the growth in `docs/size/`, and P11.06 re-locks them all with the final figures, once exp, log and pow have replaced the shared strict cores. | The table and polynomial sizes were set by instruction counts on armv7e-m-dp: a 32-entry table and degree 4 cost 2 instructions per call more, and exp2 would have missed AOR's count. The slow path is D187's, moved into three hidden functions of their own in `libc/math/fast/` (`__libc_fast_exp_special` and its exp2 and exp10 forms) so that both layers share it; as `static` functions in `math-fast.h` they were emitted at -O0 into the float-only layers, whose check refuses double helpers. Instructions per call: `exp` 3813 → 317 on armv7e-m (AOR 1090) and 10,317 → 1410 on armv6-m (AOR 3954); `exp2` 5235 → 281 (AOR 934) and 14,133 → 1210 (AOR 3335). `expm1` keeps D187's file until P11.05c2, since it needs relative accuracy where the kernel's terms cancel. | Fixed point instead of D187's double file over the soft-double helpers (`exp` 1170 instructions on armv7e-m, 3648 on armv6-m, both above AOR): a 64-bit significand holds the double's 53 bits with 11 to spare, so the error before the one rounding stays below 2^-9 ulp with no double-double pair. |
| D189 | **Fast `expm1` on the soft-double cores in 64-bit fixed point** (P11.05c2, `docs/research/libm-fast-double.md` §8). `libc/math/dp-fixed/fast/expm1.c`. Below 1/16, x (1 + q) with q = x/2 + x²/6 + … in Q64 from y = 16|x| and nine coefficients 2^-4k/(k + 1)!, by unsigned Horner's rule that subtracts for x < 0, so the result is relative however small x is; from 1/16, D188's kernel result in Q63 (its polynomial now an inline, `__libc_dp_fixed_exp2_fraction`, that `__libc_dp_fixed_exp2` wraps) minus 1 exactly in integers, where the result is at least 0.06 and the kernel's few units of 2^-63 stay below 0.03 ulp. Instructions per call: 4075 → 346 on armv7e-m (P11.05b's file 3549) and 10,938 → 1468 on armv6-m (9720); `exp`, `exp2` and `exp10` one fewer on the Thumb-2 cores. FreeBSD's per-range constants and AOR's table of 2^(i/64) − 1 avoid a cancelling subtraction in floating point; in integers the subtraction is exact, so only the kernel's absolute error had to be kept off small results. |

Measured on 10^9 samples per function on both paths: at most 0.5249 ulp; powers of 2 and 10 exact, +0 for 1 in every direction. After that re-lock, P11.06 also compares each core's fast `math-mix` and `math-float` with the fast flavour's original rule, +20 % over the speed figure (`budget.json` `program_margins_percent`). A core still over it goes to the maintainer as a decision before the phase exit; it is not re-locked silently. At P11.05d armv7e-m-dp's `math-mix` was +54.5 % over speed (12,174 → 18,806 bytes), the other Arm cores +11 to +16 %. | A re-lock alone holds the budget by definition, so without this check the growth P11.05 allowed would be accepted whatever its size.
